\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cross}}
that does not directly employ the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality can result in faster convergence rates; see for instance Proposition
S35
.
Our main result in this section is as follows:

Theorem 7
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a class of distributions satisfying Assumption
4
, and let
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n}):=\{P\in\mathcal{P}:\tau_{P}\geq\epsilon_{n}\}
, where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n}\cdot n^{\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\rightarrow\infty.

(17)

Further, assume that the tuning parameters are chosen such that
K_{X}\asymp n^{\frac{2d_{X}}{4s+d}}
and
K_{Z}\asymp\widetilde{K}_{Z}\asymp n^{\frac{2d_{Z}}{4s+d}}
and that
r\geq s\geq 3d/4
. If
T
is computed using spline regressions according to Algorithm
2
, then

\displaystyle\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(T>z_{1-\alpha})\rightarrow 1.

Theorem
7
reveals that when using spline regressions and Algorithm
2
, the PCM has uniform asymptotic power 1 over a class of alternatives that are sufficiently separated from the null, as defined by
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})
.

We remark that in Theorem
7
, we have operated in the context of a known smoothness parameter
s
for theoretical purposes. It is possible to construct more involved tests that adapt to unknown smoothness levels following the strategy of
Lepskiǐ, 1991
and
Ingster, 2000
, but we do not pursue this direction further.

The separation rate (
17
) cannot be improved further from a minimax perspective, as illustrated by the following lower bound result.

Proposition 8
.

Consider a class of distributions, denoted by
\mathcal{P}
, that satisfy Assumption
4
, let
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n}):=\{P\in\mathcal{P}:\tau_{P}\geq\epsilon_{n}\}
and let
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. There exists
c>0
such that if
\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\epsilon_{n}\cdot n^{\frac{4s}{4s+d}}<c
, then any test
\phi
having uniform asymptotic size at most
\alpha
satisfies

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\phi=1)\leq\alpha+1/2.

Proposition
8
complements Theorem
7
by showing that when
\tau_{P}
is a small constant multiple of
n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}
, no test can achieve uniform consistency under Hölder smoothness.
The proof of Proposition
8
, which can be found in Section
S1.8
, follows a fairly standard argument
(
Ingster, 1987
;
Arias-Castro et al., 2018
, e.g.)
that bounds the
\chi^{2}
-divergence from a fixed null distribution to a mixture of distributions in the alternative class
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})
.

It can be shown that under Assumption
4
, it is possible to satisfy the conditions of Assumption
3
using spline regressions with
K_{X},K_{Z}
and
\widetilde{K}_{Z}
, as well as
r
and
s
, as in Theorem
7
. Therefore, Theorem
7
and Proposition
8
reveal that assumption (d) of Theorem
5
can be the primary restriction on the separation rate
\epsilon_{n}
.

Despite the theoretical benefits described above, using Algorithm
2
instead of Algorithm
1
may degrade the practical performance of the algorithm, especially in small-sample scenarios. We therefore recommend using the PCM as in Algorithm
1
(in conjunction with Algorithm
1DR
for derandomisation), and its finite-sample performance is investigated in the next section.

6
Numerical experiments

In this section, we present the results of several simulation experiments that investigate the empirical performances of both the recommended derandomised version of the PCM (see Algorithm
1DR
) with
B=6
splits, and the version in Algorithm
1
. We compare our tests to various conditional (mean) independence tests in the literature listed below.

gam

The test based on the default
p
-value for a smooth when fitting a generalised additive model (GAM) using the
mgcv
-package in R
(
Wood, 2013
;
Wood, 2017
)
.

wgsc

The test resulting from applying the approach described in
Williamson et al., 2023
and employing sample splitting and cross-fitting as implemented in the
cv_vim
function from the
vimp
-package in R (with
K=2
, resulting in
4
folds).

kci

The
kernel conditional independence test

(
Zhang et al., 2011
)
as implemented in the
KCI
function of the
CondIndTests
R package
(
Heinze-Deml et al., 2018
)
; we use the Bayesian hyperparameter tuning option for sample sizes of at most
500
for computational stability reasons, and otherwise we use the default parameters.

gcm

The
Generalised Covariance Measure
(GCM) as described in
Shah and Peters, 2020
.

wgcm.fix

The ‘fixed weight function’ variant of the
Weighted Generalised Covariance Measure
(wGCM)
(
Scheidegger et al., 2022
)
as implemented in the
wgcm.fix
function of the
weightedGCM
R package; we use
\texttt{weight.num}=7
as in the simulations of the original paper.

wgcm.est

The ‘estimated weight function’ variant of the wGCM as implemented in the
wgcm.est
function of the
weightedGCM
R-package.

In all of our numerical simulations, rejection rates were estimated based on 2500 repetitions. The code for all of our experiments (including those in the supplementary material) is available on GitHub:
https://github.com/ARLundborg/pcm_code/
.

6.1
Additive models

We first investigate Type I error control in settings where both
{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)
and
{\mathbb{E}}(X\,|\,Z)
are additive functions, and
Z\sim N_{7}(0,\boldsymbol{I})
. For the methods, including the PCM, requiring choices of regression procedures, we use a generalised additive model fitted using
mgcv
. We employ default parameters for the generalised additive models (as given in the
smooth.terms
and
gam
functions in the
mgcv
package) except that we choose
\lfloor(N-1)/d\rfloor
basis functions (where
N
and
d
are the number of observations and predictors on which the model is trained, respectively). Since this is the largest number of basis functions per coordinate that can be taken without overparametrisation, this mitigates the risk of oversmoothing; as the fits are penalised, there is little risk of overfitting
(
Wood, 2017
)
. For the
\widetilde{v}
regression in the PCM, we apply a generalised additive model with logarithmic link function. We consider null settings consisting of
n\in\{250,500,1000\}
independent and identically distributed copies of
(X,Y,Z)
where

X=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+0.1\xi\quad\text{and}\quad Y=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+\varepsilon.

and errors
\varepsilon
and
\xi
are independent
N(0,1)
random variables, independent of
Z
. Such a setup is challenging for Type I error control as
X
and
Y
are highly correlated yet are conditionally independent given
Z
. Indeed we see from the left panel of Figure
2
that several of the tests are anti-conservative, most notably
kci
and
gam
, which we omit from further comparisons as their power properties would be hard to interpret given the high rejection rates under the null. The
wgcm.est
test is also somewhat anti-conservative, but considerably less so. In contrast, the derandomised PCM is conservative here. This is to be expected as the calibration following the derandomisation involved in its construction (Section
2.2.3
) is typically conservative; the single split version appears to have rejection rates close to the nominal 5% mark for large
n
, as suggested by our theory.

We investigate the power properties of the PCM in the following settings, where as before,
\varepsilon
and
\xi
are independent and independent of
Z
, and moreover
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
.

1.

\xi\sim N(0,1)
,
X=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+\xi
and
Y=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+0.2X^{2}+\varepsilon
.

2.

\xi+1\sim\textrm{Exp}(1)
,
X=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})-\sin(2\pi Z_{1})\xi
and
Y=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+0.4X^{2}+\varepsilon
.

3.

\xi\sim N(0,1)
,
X=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+\xi
and
Y=\sin(2\pi Z_{1})+0.4X^{2}Z_{2}+\varepsilon
.

The settings are chosen such that in setting 1:
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl({\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)\bigr)=0
but
{\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)\neq 0
, in setting 2:
{\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)=0
but
\tau\neq 0
and in setting 3: like setting 1,
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl({\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)\bigr)=0
and
{\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)\neq 0
, but there is only an interaction effect.

From the right-hand panels of Figure
2
, we see that the PCM and
wgsc
exhibit good power in settings 1 and 2.

wgcm.est
also shows appreciable power in setting 1, though as expected has little power in setting 2 where
{\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)=0
. It is initially somewhat surprising that the PCM has some power in setting 3 as the alternative is not an additive model, so the
\widehat{g}
regression is not able to correctly learn
g
. However, due to the estimation of
\widehat{v}
, we do in fact see some power for sufficiently large
n
.

Figure 2:
Rejection rates in the various settings considered in Section
6.1
looking at additive models for nominal 5%-level tests. Note that Alternative 3 has only an interaction effect, so we cannot expect methods that fit additive models to have power.

6.2
Non-additive models

In this section, we consider settings where the regression functions are non-additive and involve complex interactions. We use random forests
(
Breiman, 2001
)
implemented in the
ranger
R package
(
Wright and Ziegler, 2017
)
as our regression procedure for the methods considered. We use
500
trees and set
mtry
equal to the number of predictors, with other tuning parameters set to be the defaults for the
ranger
function; the choice of
mtry
was made as this tended to give the smallest prediction errors in our preliminary experiments.

We consider null settings consisting of
n\in\{10^{4},2\cdot 10^{4},4\cdot 10^{4}\}
independent and identically distributed copies of
(X,Y,Z)
where
Z\sim N_{7}(0,\boldsymbol{I})
as before,

X=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+\xi\quad\text{and}\quad Y=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+v(X)\varepsilon

with
\varepsilon
and
\xi
independent
N(0,1)
random variables independent of
Z
, and
v(X):=0.5+\mathbbm{1}_{\{X>0\}}
giving heteroscedastic errors for the
Y
regression model.
The larger sample sizes considered here reflect the difficulty of estimating the more complicated regression functions in these examples.
Note that here we do not have
X\mbox{${}\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp{}$}Y\,|\,Z
, but the conditional mean independence
{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)={\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)
does hold. The results are presented in Figure
3
. We see that the derandomised version of the PCM maintains Type I error control, and is in fact slightly conservative. The
wgsc
test is highly conservative. All other approaches considered appear to be anti-conservative to varying degrees, although this behaviour does appear to improve with increasing sample size.

Next we consider the following alternative settings, where as in Section
6.1
, setting 1 has
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl({\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)\bigr)=0
, setting 2 has
{\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)=0
, and setting 3 has
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl({\mathrm{Cov}}(X,Y\,|\,Z)\bigr)=0
while also involving a pure interaction effect:

1.

\xi\sim N(0,1)
,
X=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+\xi
and
Y=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+0.04X^{2}+v(X)\varepsilon
;

2.

\xi\sim\textrm{Exp}(1)
,
X=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})-\sin(\pi Z_{1})(\xi-1)
and
Y=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+0.04X^{2}+v(X)\varepsilon
;

3.

\xi\sim N(0,1)
,
X=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+\xi
and
Y=\sin(\pi Z_{1})(1+Z_{3})+0.04X^{2}Z_{2}+v(X)\varepsilon
.

Among the methods considered, here only the PCM appear to have good power across the settings considered. Despite the fact that the derandomised PCM is conservative, it still obtains greater power in some settings than the slightly anti-conservative single-split version. The
wgcm.est
has reasonable power in setting 1, though this should be interpreted with some care given that Type I error is not well controlled in the null settings. However in setting 2,
wgcm.est
is powerless as expected.

Figure 3:
Rejection rates in the various settings considered in Section
6.2
on non-additive models for nominal 5%-level tests.

7
Conclusion

In this work we have introduced a general test statistic called the PCM for testing conditional mean independence that: (a) can leverage machine learning methods to yield provable uniform Type I error control across a class of null distributions where these methods have sufficiently good predictive ability; and (b) when used in conjunction with appropriate regression methods attains rate-optimal power in both the parametric setting of the linear model and fully nonparametric settings. We believe the PCM fills an important gap in the data analyst’s range of existing tools, which are unable simultaneously to achieve these desiderata. However, our work also offers several avenues for further work, some of which we mention below.

Verifying the general assumptions for other regression methods

We have verified Assumption
3
for linear regression in linear model settings, and nonparametric series estimators in fully nonparametric settings. Since we used the penalised regression splines of
mgcv
in several of our numerical experiments, it would be interesting to see for what classes
\mathcal{P}
of distributions Assumption
3
is satisfied in that context. Similarly, it would be very interesting to ask the same question of random forests, which perform very well in our simulations; however this is likely to be challenging given the complex nature of the random forest procedure.

Conditional independence testing

Although the problem of testing conditional independence has been studied more intensively than that of testing conditional mean independence, there do not exist many practical conditional independence tests that achieve the two desiderata mentioned at the beginning of this section. One starting point for constructing such a test may be the fact that the conditional independence null
Y\mbox{${}\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp{}$}X\,|\,Z
may be viewed as the intersection of conditional mean independence nulls
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(w(Y)\,|\,X,Z\bigr)={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(w(Y)\,|\,Z\bigr)
where function
w
ranges over all monotone functions, for example. It might therefore be interesting to investigate procedures that seek two ‘projections’: mappings
(X,Z)\to\widehat{f}(X,Z)
and also
Y\mapsto\widehat{w}(Y)
, after which one may apply the GCM.

Confidence intervals

We have focused on the problem of testing conditional mean independence, but the problem of deriving confidence intervals for a parameter such as
\tau
that is
0
under our null is equally interesting. The pioneering work of
Williamson et al., 2021
proposes an asymptotically optimal approach for this in the case where
\tau
is bounded away from
0
. It would be interesting if the PCM could be used in conjunction with the proposal of
Williamson et al., 2021
to extend the latter to yield confidence intervals with uniform coverage for all
\tau
.

In Sections
S1
and
S2
of the supplementary material, we include the proofs of all of our main results and related auxiliary lemmas. In Section
S3
, we present a detailed discussion of the test proposed by
Williamson et al., 2023
. In Section
S4
, we give a self-contained description of spline regression and related results that we use for our analysis in Section
5
. Section
S5
contains an analysis of the linear projections in Section
3
under more general assumptions. We also derive an exact asymptotic power function of our test in this setting.
Section
S6
contains the results from additional numerical experiments beyond those included in Section
6
.

Throughout the supplementary material, for a positive semi-definite matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, we write
\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}
for its generalised inverse (i.e. its Moore–Penrose pseudo-inverse).

Appendix S1
Proofs

In our proofs we often suppress the dependence of quantities on
P
for ease of notation.

S1.1
Proof of Proposition
1

There is no loss of generality in assuming that
\alpha\leq 1/2
, because for
\alpha>1/2
, we have
\mathbb{P}(T>z_{1-\alpha})\geq\mathbb{P}(T>z_{1/2})=\mathbb{P}(T>0)
. We start by checking the assumptions of Lemma
S10
for the regressions of
Y
on
X
and
Z
(of which
\widehat{\beta}
is one component),
Y
on
Z
(yielding
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\theta}}
) and
X
on
Z
(yielding
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\eta}}
). Let
\delta^{\prime}:=\delta/2
.

Recalling that
W=(X,Z)
, we see that condition (i) of Lemma
S10
is satisfied for the
Y
on
X
and
Z
regression by our assumption on
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{XZ}
and the fact that

\lambda_{\min}\bigl({\mathbb{E}}(WW^{\top}\zeta^{2})\bigr)\geq c\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{XZ})

by our assumption that
{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)\geq c
. Condition (ii) is satisfied with
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and Jensen’s inequality.

By Assumption
1
, condition (i) of Lemma
S10
is satisfied for the
X
on
Z
regression. To see that condition (ii) is satisfied with
\delta=\delta^{\prime}
, we note that by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality it suffices to check that
{\mathbb{E}}(|\xi|^{4+\delta})
is bounded over
\mathcal{P}
. Letting
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}:={\mathbb{E}}(ZZ^{\top})
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}(|\xi|^{4+\delta})\leq 2^{3+\delta}\Bigl({\mathbb{E}}(|X|^{4+\delta})+\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})^{-(4+\delta)}\|{\mathbb{E}}(XZ)\|_{2}^{4+\delta}{\mathbb{E}}(\|Z\|_{2}^{4+\delta})\Bigr)

which is bounded under Assumption
1
.

To see that condition (i) of Lemma
S10
is satisfied for the
Y
on
Z
regression, define
\boldsymbol{\theta}:={\mathbb{E}}(ZZ^{\top})^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}(YZ)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and note that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(ZZ^{\top}(Y-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}Z)^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(ZZ^{\top}(\zeta+\beta X-\beta\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}Z)^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}(ZZ^{\top}\zeta^{2})+\beta^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(ZZ^{\top}(X-\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}Z)^{2}\bigr),

so the minimum eigenvalue of
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(ZZ^{\top}(Y-\boldsymbol{\theta}^{\top}Z)^{2}\bigr)
is bounded below by
c\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
. Condition (ii) follows by similar arguments as those for the
X
on
Z
regression. We therefore deduce from Lemma
S10
that (
S53
) holds with
\sigma_{\beta}^{2}
given by the
(1,1)
th entry of the matrix
(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{XZ})^{-1}\mathbb{E}(WW^{\top}\zeta^{2})(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{XZ})^{-1}
, and that

\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(S1)

We now verify that the remaining parts of Assumption
S1
are satisfied. First,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\xi_{i}\Bigr\|_{2}^{2}\biggr)=\frac{1}{n}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\|Z\xi\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{n}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}(\|Z\|_{2}^{2})\cdot\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}(\xi^{2})\to 0,

so by Lemma
S6
and (
S1
) we have that (
S54
) holds. Similar arguments show that (
S55
) is satisfied.

Next, (
S1
) shows that

\sqrt{n}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\eta}}-\boldsymbol{\eta}\|_{2}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\theta}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}\|_{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1/2})=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

Moreover, by (
S31
) in the proof of Lemma
S10
and Assumption
1
, we have

\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\Bigl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\Bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

so (
S56
) is satisfied. The remaining conditions hold by similar arguments using the moment bounds established earlier, (
S1
) and Lemma
S9
.

To verify the remaining conditions of Proposition
S37
, we note that

{\mathrm{Var}}(\varepsilon\xi)\geq{\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{{\mathrm{Var}}(\varepsilon\xi\,|\,X,Z)\bigr\}={\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{\xi^{2}{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)\bigr\}\geq c^{2}.

Finally, the moment bound condition in Proposition
S37
follows by Cauchy–Schwarz and the arguments above. The conclusion now follows from Proposition
S37
together with the fact that
\psi_{\alpha,n}
is an increasing function of
|\beta|
, so that

\psi_{\alpha,n}\geq\Phi\biggl(\frac{\kappa}{\sigma_{\beta}}\biggr)\cdot\Phi\biggl(z_{\alpha}+\frac{\kappa\sigma_{\xi}^{2}}{\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi}}\biggr)+\Phi\biggl(-\frac{\kappa}{\sigma_{\beta}}\biggr)\cdot\Phi\biggl(z_{\alpha}-\frac{\kappa\sigma_{\xi}^{2}}{\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi}}\biggr)\rightarrow 1

as
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
, as required.

S1.2
Proof of Proposition
2

Throughout this proof we work on the event that at least one
u_{n,1},\ldots,u_{n,n}
is non-zero which is a set of uniform asymptotic probability
1
by Assumption
2
(b). Let
\boldsymbol{Z}:=(Z_{1}^{\top},\ldots,Z_{n}^{\top})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{P}:=\boldsymbol{Z}(\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z})^{-1}\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{Y}:=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}:=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}
,
\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}}:=\bigl(\widehat{f}(X_{1},Z_{1}),\dots,\widehat{f}(X_{n},Z_{n})\bigr)^{\top}
and
\boldsymbol{I}
denote the
d\times d
identity matrix. Since
\boldsymbol{P}
is a matrix representing an orthogonal projection such that
\boldsymbol{Z}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{P})
is a zero vector, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{Y_{i}-\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\top}Z_{i}\}\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{P})\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{\widehat{f}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{P})\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}.

Based on the above identity, we have that

\displaystyle T=\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\top}Z_{i})^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}-\bigl(\frac{1}{n\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}\bigr)^{2}}},

where

\nu:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i})u_{n,i}^{2}}>0.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{n}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\widehat{f}
and
\bigl((X_{i},Z_{i})\bigr)_{i=1}^{n}
. Form the triangular array

W_{n,i}:=\frac{\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}}{\nu}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[n]
, and note that this satisfies the first three assumptions of Lemma
S8
, since
u_{n,i}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Finally, the fourth assumption of this lemma is also satisfied, because

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta/2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}(|W_{n,i}|^{2+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})=\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta/2}\nu^{2+\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}(|\varepsilon_{i}|^{2+\delta}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i})|u_{n,i}|^{2+\delta}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{c^{1+\delta/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\upsilon_{n,i}|^{2+\delta}\leq\frac{C}{c^{1+\delta/2}}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\upsilon_{n,i}|^{2}\biggr)\max_{i\in[n]}|\upsilon_{n,i}|^{\delta}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by Assumptions
2
(a) and (b) and Lemmas
S3
and
S7
. Lemma
S8
thus yields that the numerator of
T
is uniformly asymptotically standard Gaussian.

For the denominator of
T
, the uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem
(
Bengs and Holzmann, 2019
, Theorem 6.3)
yields that
\frac{1}{n\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
.
For the first term in the denominator of
T
, we consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\top}Z_{i})^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}}_{\textup{I}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{\top}Z_{i}\}^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}}_{\textup{II}_{n}}

\displaystyle-\underbrace{\frac{2}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{\top}Z_{i}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}^{2}}_{\textup{III}_{n}}.

Similarly to our previous argument, define the triangular array
\tilde{W}_{n,i}:=W_{n,i}^{2}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[n]
, and note that
(\tilde{W}_{n,i})_{n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n]}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
S9
with
\mu_{n}=1
in that result, so
\textup{I}_{n}=1+o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
. Now, by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle|\textup{II}_{n}|\leq\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Z_{i}\|_{\infty}^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{c}\max_{i\in[n]}\|Z_{i}\|_{\infty}^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{1}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\upsilon_{n,i}^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

by Assumption
2
and Lemma
S3
. Finally, the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality yields that

\displaystyle|\textup{III}_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{\top}Z_{i}\}^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}}=2\sqrt{\textup{I}_{n}}\cdot\sqrt{\textup{II}_{n}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)

by Lemmas
S3
and
S7
. The result follows by the uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem.

S1.3
Proof of Proposition
3

As in the proof of Proposition
2
, we work on the event that at least one
u_{n,1},\ldots,u_{n,n}
is non-zero, which is a set of uniform asymptotic probability
1
by Assumption
2
(b). Recall the definitions of
\nu
from the proof of Proposition
2
, and
\delta_{\mathrm{bias}}
from just after (
9
). Our test statistic can be written as

\displaystyle T=\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\delta_{\mathrm{bias}}}{\sqrt{\frac{1}{ns_{n}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\top}Z_{i})^{2}u_{n,i}^{2}-\bigl(\frac{1}{n\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}+\frac{1}{n\nu}\delta_{\mathrm{bias}}\bigr)^{2}}}.

Following the proof of Proposition
2
, we know that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}u_{n,i}
converges uniformly to
N(0,1)
. Further, by Assumption
2
(a) and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\biggl|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\delta_{\mathrm{bias}}\biggr|\leq\frac{1}{c^{1/2}}\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma})^{\top}Z_{i}\upsilon_{n,i}\biggr|\leq\frac{1}{c^{1/2}}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{1}\biggl\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\upsilon_{n,i}\biggr\|_{\infty}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

under condition (
10
). A uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem
(
Bengs and Holzmann, 2019
, Theorem 6.3)
now yields that the numerator of
T
is uniformly asymptotically standard Gaussian. We can repeat the arguments of Proposition
2
to show that the denominator is
1+o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
under Assumption
2
, so the uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem yields the desired result.

S1.4
Proof of Theorem
4

We first prove the result under (i), (ii) or (iii). For distinct
i,j\in[n]
, let

R_{ij}:={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)
, where
M_{i}:=m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})
, and assume that

\frac{1}{n\sigma_{n}^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\bigl|{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(R_{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr)\bigr|=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1).

(S2)

In Proposition
S14
, we show that this condition is satisfied if either (i), (ii) or (iii) hold. Define
\nu^{2}:={\mathrm{Var}}(\varepsilon\xi\,|\,\widehat{f})
and note that
\nu^{2}\geq c\sigma^{2}
by assumption (c). Throughout this proof we work on the event
\Omega_{0}:=\{\sigma>0\}
, which satisfies
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{0}^{c})=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
by assumption (a).
Define

T^{(\mathrm{N})}:=\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}{\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad T^{(\mathrm{D})}:=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}-\bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\bigr)^{2}}}{\nu},

so that

T=T^{(\mathrm{N})}/T^{(\mathrm{D})}
. We will show that
T^{(\mathrm{N})}
converges uniformly in distribution to
N(0,1)
and
|(T^{(\mathrm{D})})^{2}-1|=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
, which yields the desired result by combining Lemma
S7
and the uniform version of Slutsky’s lemma
(
Bengs and Holzmann, 2019
, Theorem 6.3)
.

Define
\widetilde{M}_{i}:=m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})
for
i\in[n]
and note that

T^{(\mathrm{N})}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}\widetilde{M}_{i}}_{a_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}_{b_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}\xi_{i}}_{c_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\xi_{i}}_{U_{n}}.

(S3)

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

a_{n}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{cn}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\biggr)}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1),

by Assumption
3
(b).

To see that
b_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
, we note that

b_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\widetilde{M}_{i}\widetilde{M}_{j}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}.

(S4)

By Assumption
3
(a) and (c) and assumption (c) of the theorem, the first term in (
S4
) satisfies

{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\biggm|(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f},\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}\biggr)\leq\frac{C}{nc\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

so the same is true unconditionally by Lemma
S6
. Now, for
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{i}Y_{j}\,|\,X_{i},X_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j})={\mathbb{E}}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i}){\mathbb{E}}(Y_{j}\,|\,X_{j},Z_{j})=m(Z_{i})m(Z_{j}),

using the fact that
m(Z)={\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)={\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)
under
\mathcal{P}_{0}
. Hence,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\,|\,X_{i},X_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{\bigl(Y_{i}-m(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(Y_{j}-m(Z_{j})\bigr)\,|\,X_{i},X_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j}\bigr\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}(Y_{i}Y_{j}\,|\,X_{i},X_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j})-m(Z_{i})m(Z_{j})=0.

It follows that the second term in (
S4
) satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\widetilde{M}_{i}\widetilde{M}_{j}\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}

\displaystyle\biggm|(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f},\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\widetilde{M}_{i}\widetilde{M}_{j}{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\,|\,X_{i},X_{j},Z_{i},Z_{j})=0,

and we deduce by Lemmas
S6
and
S7
that
b_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
.

To see that
c_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
, we proceed as above and write

\displaystyle c_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\xi_{i}^{2}+\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}M_{i}M_{j}\xi_{i}\xi_{j},

where we note that the first term is
o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
by Assumption
3
(b). Moreover,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}M_{i}M_{j}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\biggm|(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}{\mathbb{E}}(R_{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i=1}^{n},\widehat{f}),

where the last equality holds since

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr){\mathbb{E}}(\xi_{i}\,|\,Z_{i},\widehat{f}){\mathbb{E}}(\xi_{j}\,|\,Z_{j},\widehat{f})

\displaystyle=0.

Continuing, we have by (
S2
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(R_{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{cn\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\big|{\mathbb{E}}(R_{ij}\xi_{n,i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f})\big|=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1).

Therefore, by Lemmas
S6
and
S7
we conclude that
c_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
as desired.

To deal with the final term, we define the triangular array

W_{n,i}:=\nu^{-1}\varepsilon_{i}\xi_{i}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[n]
, and note that
W_{n,i}
satisfies all the conditions of Lemma
S8
by assumptions (b) and (c) (here we condition on
\widehat{f}
in applying this result). Hence,
U_{n}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}W_{n,i}
, and therefore
T^{(\mathrm{N})}
, converges uniformly in distribution to
N(0,1)
.

We now show that
|(T^{(\mathrm{D})})^{2}-1|=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
, from which the desired result follows from Lemma
S7
. Note that

(T^{(\mathrm{D})})^{2}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}}_{p_{n}}-\biggl(\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}\nu}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}}_{q_{n}}\biggr)^{2}

(S5)

and that
q_{n}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}T^{(\mathrm{N})}
. We have just shown that
T^{(\mathrm{N})}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
, so
q_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
and we are therefore done if we can show that
|p_{n}-1|=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
. Now

\displaystyle p_{n}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\xi_{i}^{2}}_{\textup{I}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}}_{\textup{II}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{4}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}\widetilde{M}_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\xi_{i}}_{\textup{III}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}_{\textup{IV}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}

(S6)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\xi_{i}^{2}}_{\textup{IV}_{n}^{\xi}}+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}M_{i}\varepsilon_{i}}_{\textup{V}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\widetilde{M}_{i}\xi_{i}}_{\textup{V}_{n}^{\xi}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}\xi_{i}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}}_{\textup{VI}_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}}_{\textup{VI}_{n}^{\xi}}.

Consider the triangular array

\tilde{W}_{n,i}:=W_{n,i}^{2}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[n]
, and note that it satisfies all the conditions of Lemma
S9
by assumptions (b) and (c) with
\mu_{n}=1
(again conditioning on
\widehat{f}
in that result), so
|\textup{I}_{n}-1|=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
. It remains to show that the remaining terms are
o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
. Now

0\leq\textup{II}_{n}\leq\frac{1}{cn}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by Assumption
3
(a). By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

|\textup{III}_{n}|\leq 4\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\xi_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}=4\textup{I}_{n}^{1/2}\textup{II}_{n}^{1/2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by the above and Lemma
S7
. Now

|\textup{IV}_{n}^{\varepsilon}|\leq\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by our argument for the first term in (
S4
). A similar argument shows that
\textup{IV}_{n}^{\xi}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
by Assumption
3
(b). By the triangle inequality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

|\textup{V}_{n}^{\varepsilon}|\leq 2\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}=2\textup{II}_{n}^{1/2}(\textup{IV}_{n}^{\varepsilon})^{1/2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by the above and Lemma
S7
. A similar argument can be used for
\textup{V}_{n}^{\xi}
. Finally, again by the triangle inequality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

|\textup{VI}_{n}^{\varepsilon}|\leq 2\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\xi_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}=2\textup{I}_{n}^{1/2}(\textup{IV}_{n}^{\varepsilon})^{1/2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by the above and Lemma
S7
;
\textup{VI}_{n}^{\xi}
is handled similarly. This proves the desired result under (i), (ii) or (iii).

To prove the result under (iv), we note that only the argument for the
c_{n}
term needs modification. That
c_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)
under the assumption that
\widehat{m}
is sufficiently stable follows immediately from Proposition
S15
and Assumption
3
(b), with
\xi_{i}
in that result taken to be
\xi_{i}/\sigma
here.

S1.5
Proof of Theorem
5

We first prove the result under assumption (i). Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, so that
z_{1-\alpha}>0
. Let
s
denote the denominator in the definition of
T
. Suppose there exists
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\leq c\tau\Bigr)\to 0,

(S7)

\displaystyle\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau).

(S8)

Note that, since
0/0:=0
and
\tau>0
, we have that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(T\leq z_{1-\alpha})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\leq z_{1-\alpha}\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\leq c\tau\biggr)+{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(z_{1-\alpha}\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}\tau}\geq c\biggr).

Thus, from (
S7
) and (
S8
),

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}(T\leq z_{1-\alpha})\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}\Biggl\{{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\leq c\tau\biggr)+{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(z_{1-\alpha}\frac{s_{n}}{\sqrt{n}\tau}\geq c\biggr)\Biggr\}\to 0

and hence the result will follow if we can prove (
S7
) and (
S8
).

Observe that if we define

\check{f}(X,Z):=\frac{\tau^{1/2}}{\sigma}\widehat{f}(X,Z)

(S9)

and let
\check{T}
denote the test using
\check{f}
in place of
\widehat{f}
, then
T=\check{T}
, since we have assumed in (a) that
m_{\widehat{f}}
is scale equivariant. It follows that we may put
\check{f}
in place of
\widehat{f}
and assume without loss of generality that

\sigma^{2}={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\xi^{2}\,|\,\widehat{f}\bigr)=\tau.

For both claims (
S7
) and (
S8
), we therefore work with
\check{f}
instead of
\widehat{f}
,
\check{\xi}:=\check{f}(X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}(\check{f}(X,Z)\,|\,\check{f},Z)
instead of
\xi
and similarly
\check{\xi}_{i}
instead of
\xi_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.

To prove (
S7
), we write
Y_{i}=m(Z_{i})+h(X_{i},Z_{i})+\zeta_{i}
, and have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(X_{i},Z_{i})\check{\xi}_{i}}_{\textup{I}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\check{\xi}_{i}}_{\textup{II}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}\check{\xi}_{i}}_{\textup{III}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(X_{i},Z_{i})\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{IV}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{V}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{VI}_{n}}.

Defining the triangular array
W_{n,i}:=h(X_{i},Z_{i})\check{\xi}_{i}/\tau
for
i\in[n]
, note by assumption (b) that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{2}\,|\,\check{f}\bigr)=\frac{n{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(h(X,Z)^{2}\check{\xi}^{2}\,|\,\check{f}\bigr)}{\tau^{2}}\leq\frac{C^{2}n}{\tau}.

Therefore, defining
\mu_{n}:={\mathbb{E}}(\textup{I}_{n}\,|\,\check{f})
(the numerator of
\mathrm{Corr}(h(X,Z),\check{\xi}\,|\,\check{f})
), assumption (ii) of Lemma
S9
is satisfied with
\delta=1
on
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})
by assumption (c). We deduce that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}(|\textup{I}_{n}-\mu_{n}|\geq\eta\tau)=o(1)

for any
\eta>0
.

To deal with the
\textup{II}_{n}
term, we first note that for
i\neq j
,

{\mathbb{E}}(\zeta_{i}\zeta_{j}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i},X_{j},Z_{j})={\mathbb{E}}(\zeta_{i}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i}){\mathbb{E}}(\zeta_{j}\,|\,X_{j},Z_{j})=0.

Hence, using the fact that
{\mathbb{E}}(\zeta_{i}^{2}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i})={\mathrm{Var}}(Y_{i}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i})\leq C
for all
i\in[n]
by Assumption
3
(c), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|\textup{II}_{n}|\,|\,\check{f},(X_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}(\zeta_{i}^{2}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i})\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2},

and therefore

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|\textup{II}_{n}|\,|\,\check{f}\bigr)\leq\frac{C^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\tau^{1/2}.

We conclude by Lemma
S2
that
\textup{II}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(n^{-1/2}\tau^{1/2})
. To deal with
\textup{III}_{n}
, we first write

\textup{III}_{n}^{2}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\check{\xi}_{i}\check{\xi}_{j}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}\{m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}(Z_{j})\}

and note that the first term is
o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau n^{-1})
by Assumption
3
(b). For the second term, note similarly to
\textup{II}_{n}
that

{\mathbb{E}}(\check{\xi}_{i}\check{\xi}_{j}\,|\,\check{f},Z_{i},Z_{j})={\mathbb{E}}(\check{\xi}_{i}\,|\,\check{f},Z_{i}){\mathbb{E}}(\check{\xi}_{j}\,|\,\check{f},Z_{j})=0.

(S10)

Thus, since
\widehat{m}
is formed on auxiliary data in Algorithm
2
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n\nu^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\check{\xi}_{i}\check{\xi}_{j}\,|\,\check{f},Z_{i},Z_{j}

\displaystyle\biggm|\check{f},(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{m}\biggr)=0.

We deduce by Lemma
S6
that
\textup{III}_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2})
. Next, we note that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}=\frac{\tau}{n\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau),

(S11)

by Assumption
3
(a). By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, Markov’s inequality for the first factor and (
S11
) for the second factor, we have

|\textup{IV}_{n}|\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(X_{i},Z_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau).

For the
\textup{V}_{n}
term, we note that by similar arguments as above,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|\textup{V}_{n}|\,|\,\check{f},(X_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\bigr)\leq\frac{C^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2}).

(S12)

Hence by Lemma
S2
, we deduce that
\textup{V}_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2})
.
For the final term, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle|\textup{VI}_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2})

using Assumptions
3
(a) and (
S11
). Letting
R_{n}:=\textup{II}_{n}+\textup{III}_{n}+\textup{IV}_{n}+\textup{V}_{n}+\textup{VI}_{n}
, we have therefore shown that
R_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau)
by assumption (c). We conclude that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{n,i}\leq\rho\tau/3\Bigr)\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}(\mu_{n}\leq\rho\tau)

\displaystyle+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}(|\textup{I}_{n}-\mu_{n}|\geq\rho\tau/3)

\displaystyle+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}(|R_{n}|\geq\rho\tau/3),

so (
S7
) is satisfied with
c:=\rho/3
by assumption (d).

To see that (
S8
) holds, note that

\displaystyle\frac{s_{n}}{n^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\leq 5^{1/2}\biggl[\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(X_{i},Z_{i})^{2}\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{I}}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}^{2}\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{II}}_{n}}+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{III}}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(X_{i},Z_{i})^{2}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{IV}}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}^{2}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{V}}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\{m_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\check{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{VI}}_{n}}\biggr].

Now

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\widetilde{\textup{I}}_{n}\,|\,\check{f}\bigr)\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\check{\xi}_{i}^{2}\,|\,\check{f}\bigr)\biggr)^{1/2}=\frac{C}{n^{1/2}}\tau^{1/2},

so by Lemma
S2
we see that
\widetilde{\textup{I}}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(n^{-1/2}\tau^{1/2})
. The remaining terms are of the same uniform stochastic order as the corresponding terms without tildes using the bounds above. Thus, (
S8
) is satisfied by assumption (c), and the result follows.

If we assume as in (ii) that the test statistic is computed via Algorithm
1
and that
\widehat{m}
is sufficiently stable, then the only changes to the above proof are when dealing with the
\textup{III}_{n}
term.
But this remains
o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2})
by the stability assumptions on
\widehat{m}
and Proposition
S15
.

S1.6
Proof of Theorem
6

It suffices to verify that Assumption
3
holds and to check assumptions (a)-(c) of Theorem
4
as the conclusion then follows from Theorem
4
.

To see that assumption (a) of Theorem
4
holds, note that by Proposition
S31
with
\widehat{f}
in place of
f
in that result,

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle:=\widehat{f}(X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{\widehat{f}(X,Z)\,|\,Z,\widehat{f}\bigr\}=(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}})^{\top}\bigl\{\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)\,|\,Z\bigr)\bigr\}.

(S13)

Thus, from the definition in (
12
),

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}={\mathbb{E}}(\xi^{2}\,|\,\widehat{f})=(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}})\geq\tilde{\lambda}_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda})\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq cK^{-1}_{XZ}\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2},

(S14)

where the last inequality holds by our assumption (
14
). Hence,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}\mathbb{P}(\sigma^{2}=0)=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{0}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}=0)=o(1),

as desired.

Next, for any
\eta\geq 1
, we have by (
S13
) that

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|\xi|^{\eta}\,|\,Z,\widehat{f}\bigr)\leq 2^{\eta}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\bigl|(\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)\bigr|^{\eta}\,|\,Z,\widehat{f}\bigr)\leq 2^{\eta}\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{\eta}

(S15)

by Hölder’s inequality and Proposition
S23
(a).
By (
S14
), Assumption
4
(a), (
S15
) with
\eta=2+\delta
and (
16
), we have

\frac{{\mathbb{E}}(|\xi\varepsilon|^{2+\delta}\,|\,\widehat{f})}{\sigma^{2+\delta}}\leq\frac{2^{2+\delta}C}{c^{2+\delta}}\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{2+\delta}}{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2+\delta}}K_{XZ}^{1+\delta/2}\leq\frac{2^{2+\delta}C}{c^{2+\delta}}K_{XZ}^{1+\delta/2}=o(n^{\delta/2}),

so assumption (b) of Theorem
4
is satisfied. Assumption (c) of Theorem
4
is also a hypothesis of the result we seek to prove (see Assumption
4
), so there is nothing to check.

To see that Assumption
3
(a) holds, we first define
M_{i}:=m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})
and
\widetilde{M}_{i}:=m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})
for
i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}
. By Corollary
S30
and (
15
),

\mathcal{E}_{P,1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}^{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\bigl(\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-2s/d_{Z}}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}/n\bigr)=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1).

(S16)

Now, suppose that
g^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{XZ}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\phi}
is the
L_{2}(P)
-best approximant of
g
over
\mathcal{S}_{r,L}^{d}
. Then, by Propositions
S31
and
S23
(b),

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}_{XZ}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}_{XZ}\|_{\infty}

(S17)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}_{XZ}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{XZ}\|_{\infty}+2c_{s}(r)^{-d}\|g-g^{\dagger}\|_{\infty}+2c_{s}(r)^{-d}\|g\|_{\infty}.

Hence, by Corollary
S30
, Propositions
S26
and
S27
and Assumption
4
,

\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(K_{XZ}n^{-1/2}+1)=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1),

(S18)

where the last equality uses (
16
). Now
\widetilde{m}(Z)
is in the span of
\boldsymbol{\psi}(Z)
, so the residuals
m_{\widehat{f}}-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}
are identical to those resulting from a
\widehat{g}(X,Z)
on
\boldsymbol{\psi}(Z)
regression. Thus, by Proposition
S34
, (
S14
) and (
15
),

\mathcal{E}_{P,2}=\frac{1}{n\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\biggl(\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}}K_{XZ}\{\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-2s/d_{Z}}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}/n\}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1).

(S19)

Combining (
S16
) and (
S19
), we have

\mathcal{E}_{P,1}\mathcal{E}_{P,2}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\biggl(\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}}K_{XZ}\{\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-2s/d_{Z}}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}/n\}^{2}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(n^{-1}),

by (
15
), so Assumption
3
(a) holds.

To verify Assumption
3
(b), note by Lemma
S2
that, since
{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon^{2}\,|\,X,Z)\leq{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon^{2+\delta}\,|\,X,Z)^{2/(2+\delta)}\leq C^{2/(2+\delta)}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Biggl(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\biggm|\widehat{f},(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}\Biggr)=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{M}_{i}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i})\biggr\}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\biggl(\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}}K_{XZ}\{\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-2s/d_{Z}}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}/n\}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(K_{XZ}^{1/2}n^{-1/2})=o_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(1)

by (
15
) and (
16
). Assumption
3
(c) is also the hypothesis of Assumption
4
(a), and this completes the proof.

S1.7
Proof of Theorem
7

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, so that
z_{1-\alpha}>0
. Let
s_{n}
denote the denominator in the definition of
T
. Suppose we can show that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}

\displaystyle=\tau(1+R_{n}),\quad\text{where $R_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(1)$}

(S20)

\displaystyle\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=\tau U_{n},\quad\text{where $U_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(1)$}.

(S21)

Note that, since
0/0:=0
and
\tau>0
, we have from (
S20
) and (
S21
) that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(T\leq z_{1-\alpha})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}\leq z_{1-\alpha}\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}}\biggr)={\mathbb{P}}(z_{1-\alpha}U_{n}-R_{n}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|U_{n}|\geq\frac{1}{2z_{1-\alpha}}\biggr)+{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|R_{n}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\biggr).

Thus, from (
S20
) and (
S21
),

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}(T\leq z_{1-\alpha})\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|U_{n}|\geq\frac{1}{2z_{1-\alpha}}\biggr)+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|R_{n}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\biggr)\to 0

and hence the result will follow if we can prove (
S20
) and (
S21
).

To see that (
S20
) holds, we write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(X_{i},Z_{i})}_{\textup{I}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\{m(Z_{i})-\widetilde{m}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{II}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i},Z_{i})\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{III}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{Y_{i}-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{IV}_{n}}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})}_{\textup{V}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{VI}_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{VII}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}}_{\textup{VIII}_{n}}.

Using the fact that
\bigl(\varepsilon_{i}f(X_{i},Z_{i})\bigr)_{i=1}^{n}
are independent and identically distributed with mean
\tau
, we have that

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(X_{i},Z_{i})-\tau\biggr|\biggr)\leq{\mathbb{E}}\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}f(X_{i},Z_{i})-\tau\biggr)^{2}\biggr\}^{1/2}

(S22)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}\bigl\{{\mathrm{Var}}\bigl(\varepsilon f(X,Z)\bigr)\bigr\}^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\varepsilon^{2}f(X,Z)^{2}\bigr)\bigr\}^{1/2}\leq\Bigl(\frac{C^{2/(2+\delta)}\tau}{n}\Bigr)^{1/2},

so
\textup{I}_{n}-\tau=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\tau^{1/2}/n^{1/2}\bigr)
, by Lemma
S2
.
Now note that for
i\neq j
,

{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}\,|\,Z_{i},Z_{j})={\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}\,|\,Z_{i}){\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{j}\,|\,Z_{j})=0.

(S23)

Hence, by Assumption
4
(a), we have that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|\textup{II}_{n}|\,|\,\widetilde{m},(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}(\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\,|\,Z_{i})\{m(Z_{i})-\widetilde{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{1/(2+\delta)}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widetilde{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}.

Thus, by Corollary
S30
and Lemma
S2
,

\textup{II}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(K_{Z}^{-s/d_{Z}}n^{-1/2}+K_{Z}^{1/2}n^{-1}\bigr)=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{(4s+d/2)}{4s+d}}+n^{-\frac{(4s+d_{X})}{4s+d}}\bigr)=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr).

Since
\tau={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(f(X,Z)^{2}\bigr)
, we have by Proposition
S36
that

\displaystyle\textup{III}_{n}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(K_{XZ}^{-2s/d}+K_{XZ}^{-(s/d-1/2)}n^{-1}+\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2}\{1+K_{XZ}^{-(s/d-1/2)}\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}+\tau^{1/2}n^{-1/2}\bigr).

Next, by Assumption
4
(a) and (c),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{Y-g(X,Z)\}^{2}\,|\,X,Z\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{Y-m(Z)\}^{2}\,|\,X,Z\bigr)-2m(Z)^{2}+f(X,Z)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2/(2+\delta)}+4C^{2}

and for
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{Y_{i}-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}\{Y_{j}-g(X_{j},Z_{j})\}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i},X_{j},Z_{j}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{Y_{i}-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i}\bigr){\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{Y_{j}-g(X_{j},Z_{j})\}\,|\,X_{j},Z_{j}\bigr)=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(

\displaystyle|\textup{IV}_{n}|\,|\,\widehat{g},(X_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{Y_{i}-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\,|\,X_{i},Z_{i}\bigr)\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(C^{2/(2+\delta)}+4C^{2})^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}.

By Corollary
S30
and Lemma
S2
we thus have

\textup{IV}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(K_{XZ}^{-s/d}n^{-1/2}+K_{XZ}^{1/2}n^{-1}\bigr)=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{(4s+d/2)}{4s+d}}+n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr)=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr).

Now, using (
S23
) and the fact that
m_{\widehat{f}}(Z)={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\widehat{f}(X,Z)-f(X,Z)\,|\,Z,\widehat{f}\bigr)
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(|\textup{V}_{n}|\,|\,\widehat{f},(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\bigr)\leq\frac{C^{1/(2+\delta)}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-f(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\,|\,Z_{i},\widehat{f}\bigr)\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{1/2}C^{1/(2+\delta)}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\,|\,Z_{i},\widehat{f}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{\widetilde{m}(Z_{i})-m(Z_{i})\}^{2}\,|\,Z_{i},\widehat{f}\bigr)\biggr)^{1/2}.

By Corollary
S30
and Lemma
S2
, we deduce that

\textup{V}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(K_{XZ}^{-s/d}n^{-1/2}+K_{XZ}^{1/2}n^{-1}+K_{Z}^{-s/d_{Z}}n^{-1/2}+K_{Z}^{1/2}n^{-1}\bigr)=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr).

As in the proof of Theorem
6
,
\widetilde{m}(Z)
is in the span of
\boldsymbol{\psi}(Z)
, the residuals
m_{\widehat{f}}-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}
are identical to those resulting from a
\widehat{g}(X,Z)
on
\boldsymbol{\psi}(Z)
regression. Moreover, by (
S18
),

\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(K_{XZ}n^{-1/2}+1)=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

(S24)

where the final equality uses the fact that
s\geq 3d/4
. We deduce by Proposition
S34
that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-2s/d_{Z}}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}n^{-1}).

(S25)

By a similar argument as for the
\textup{II}_{n}
term, but conditioning on
\widehat{f}
and
\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}
instead of
\widetilde{m}
and applying (
S25
), we conclude that

\textup{VI}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr).

We now intend to apply Proposition
S35
to the
\textup{VII}_{n}
term with
(X,Y,Z)=\bigl(\widehat{f}(X,Z),Y,Z\bigr)
. By (
S24
), we can choose
\sigma_{n}^{2}=\max(\|\boldsymbol{\Pi}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{2},C^{2})
to fulfill Assumption (iii) of that result, and Assumption (ii) is satisfied by Assumption
4
(b). Assumption (i) is satisfied with
\zeta_{f}=\zeta_{g}=s/d_{Z}
by Propositions
S26
and
S27
, Lemma
S33
, (
S24
) and Assumption
4
(c). We therefore have by Proposition
S35
that

\displaystyle\textup{VII}_{n}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-2s/d_{Z}}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{1/2}n^{-1}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{2-s/d_{Z}}\log(\widetilde{K}_{Z})n^{-2}\bigr)

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}+n^{-\frac{4s+d_{X}}{4s+d}}+\log(n)n^{-\frac{(10s+2d-4d_{Z})}{4s+d}})=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr)

using that
s\geq 3d/4
. For the final error term, similar to previous terms, for
i\neq j
,

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}\{\widehat{f}(X_{j},Z_{j})-m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{j})\}\,|\,\widehat{f},Z_{i},Z_{j}\bigr)=0,

so, by Hölder’s inequality and the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(

\displaystyle|\textup{VIII}_{n}|\,|\,\widehat{f},\widehat{m},(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\,|\,\widehat{f},Z_{i}\bigr)\{\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-m(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\|\widehat{g}\|_{\infty}}{n^{1/2}}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-m(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}.

Combining Proposition
S23
(b), the argument leading to (
S17
), and (
S24
) yields that
\|\widehat{g}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}_{XZ}\|_{\infty}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. We can therefore apply Corollary
S30
and Lemma
S2
as for
\textup{II}_{n}
to conclude that

\textup{VIII}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}).

Combining these bounds, we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}=\tau(1+R_{n}),

where

R_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(\tau^{-1}n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}+\tau^{-1/2}n^{-1/2}\bigr).

It follows that

R_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}\bigl(\epsilon_{n}^{-1}n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}+\epsilon_{n}^{-1/2}n^{-1/2}\bigr),

so by Lemma
S4
and (
17
), (
S20
) holds.

To see that (
S21
) holds, note that

\displaystyle\frac{s_{n}}{n^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\leq 8^{1/2}\biggl[\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}f(X_{i},Z_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{I}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\{m(Z_{i})-\widetilde{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{II}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i},Z_{i})^{2}\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{III}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{Y_{i}-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\{\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})-g(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{IV}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{V}}_{n}}+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i}^{2}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{VI}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{VII}}_{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\underbrace{\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\{\widehat{f}(X_{i},Z_{i})-m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}}_{\widetilde{\textup{VIII}}_{n}}\biggr].

Combining the bound in (
S22
) with Lemma
S2
yields that
\widetilde{\textup{I}}_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(\tau^{1/2}/n^{1/2})
. All other terms except
\widetilde{\textup{VII}}_{n}
are of the same uniform stochastic order as the same expressions for the corresponding terms without tildes. For the final term, then,

\widetilde{\textup{VII}}_{n}\leq n^{-1/2}\|\widehat{m}-m\|_{\infty}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{m_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}_{\widehat{f}}(Z_{i})\}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}.

Now
\widehat{m}=\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}
, and we can let
m^{\dagger}=\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\psi}
denote the
L_{2}(P)
-best approximant of
m
over
\mathcal{S}_{2r-1,L}^{d}
. Then by Proposition
S23
(b), Corollary
S30
, the fact that
s\geq 3d/4
, Propositions
S26
and
S27
, we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{m}-m\|_{\infty}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\gamma}}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}\|_{\infty}+\|m^{\dagger}-m\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}(\widetilde{K}_{Z}n^{-1/2}+\widetilde{K}_{Z}^{-s/d_{Z}})

\displaystyle=O_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{2d_{X}}{4s+d}}+n^{-\frac{2s}{4s+d}}\bigr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1),

so

\widetilde{\textup{VII}}_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}\bigl(n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}}\bigr)

by (
S25
). We conclude that
s_{n}/n^{1/2}=\tau U_{n}
, where

U_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(\tau^{-1/2}n^{-1/2}+\tau^{-1}n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}})=o_{\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}(1),

and hence (
S21
) is satisfied. This completes the proof.

S1.8
Proof of Proposition
8

Recall that given two probability measures
\mu
and
\nu
on a measurable space
(E,\mathcal{E})
such that
\mu
has density
p
with respect to
\nu
, we define the
\chi^{2}
-divergence from
\nu
to
\mu
by

\chi^{2}(\mu,\nu):=\int_{E}p^{2}\,d\nu-1.

Let
\mathcal{A}:=[0,1]^{d_{X}}\times\{-1,1\}\times[0,1]^{d_{Z}}
, and let
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
denote a fixed null distribution supported on
\mathcal{A}
. Further, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})
denote a finite family of alternative distributions supported on
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
has density
q_{Q}:\mathcal{A}\to[0,\infty)
with respect to
P_{0}
and define
P_{0}^{n}:=P_{0}^{\otimes n}
and

P_{1}^{n}:=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}Q^{\otimes n},

where
\otimes n
denotes the
n
-fold product of a measure with itself. Suppose that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\chi^{2}(P_{1}^{n},P_{0}^{n})\leq 1.

(S26)

Now, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and tests
\phi
,

\displaystyle\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\phi=1)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}{\mathbb{P}}_{Q}(\phi=1)\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}{\mathbb{P}}_{Q}(\phi=1)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\phi\,\mathrm{d}P_{1}^{n}\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{P_{0}}(\phi=1)+d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{0}^{n},P_{1}^{n}).

Defining
q_{Q}^{\otimes n}(x_{1},y_{1},z_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{n},z_{n}):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}q_{Q}(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})
, we have by Jensen’s inequality that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}(P_{0}^{n},P_{1}^{n})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\biggl|\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}q_{Q}^{\otimes n}-1\biggr|\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}^{n}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\biggl\{\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\biggl(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|}\sum_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}q_{Q}^{\otimes n}-1\biggr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}^{n}\biggr\}=\frac{1}{4}\chi^{2}(P_{1}^{n},P_{0}^{n}).

Thus

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\phi=1)\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{2}

for all asymptotically valid tests
\phi
, by (
S26
).

We now construct
P_{0}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
such that (
S26
) holds. We let
P_{0}
denote the uniform distribution on
\mathcal{A}
. Then

\displaystyle g_{P_{0}}(x,z)={\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}(Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)={\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}(Y)=0={\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}(Y\,|\,Z=z)=m_{P_{0}}(z),

so
\tau_{P_{0}}=0
. We also note that
g
and
m
are constant functions and so
g,m\in\mathcal{H}_{s}
with
\|m\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}=\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}=0
. It is immediate from similar arguments that the remaining conditions of Assumption
4
are satisfied for
P_{0}
, so
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
.

We now aim to construct
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
. To this end, define the bump function
K:[0,1/2]\rightarrow[0,\infty)
by
K(x):=e^{-\frac{1}{x\cdot(1/2-x)^{2}}}
, let
I_{0}:=\bigl(\int_{0}^{1/2}K(u)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u\bigr)^{1/2}\in(0,\infty)
and define
v:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by
v(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}I_{0}}\cdot K(x)\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in[0,1/2]\}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}I_{0}}\cdot K(x-1/2)\mathbbm{1}_{\{x\in[1/2,1]\}}
and
v(x):=0
for
x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus[0,1]
, so that
v
is infinitely differentiable with
v(0)=v(1)=0
,
\int_{0}^{1}v(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0
and
\int_{0}^{1}v(x)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=1
. Now define
h:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by
h(x_{1},\dots,x_{d}):=\prod_{j=1}^{d}v(x_{j})
and note that
h
is
0
outside
[0,1]^{d}
,
h
is infinitely differentiable,
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}h^{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}x_{d}=1
and
\int_{0}^{1}h(x_{1},\dots,x_{j},\dots,x_{d})\,\mathrm{d}x_{j}=0
for
j\in[d]
.

Define
\rho_{n}:=\big\lfloor{n^{\frac{2}{4s+d}}}\big\rfloor
and, for
j\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}
, define
h_{n,j}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+d_{Z}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by
h_{n,j}(x,z):=\rho_{n}^{d/2}h\bigl(\rho_{n}\cdot(x,z)-j+1\bigr)
, so that
(h_{n,j})_{j\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}}
have disjoint support,
\|h_{n,j}\|_{2}=1
and
\|h_{n,j}\|_{\infty}=\rho_{n}^{d/2}\|h\|_{\infty}
. Let
\gamma_{n}:=c^{1/2}n^{-\frac{2s+d}{4s+d}}
, where
c\in\bigl(0,\rho_{n}^{-d}\|h\|_{\infty}^{-2}\bigr)
will be specified later. For
\boldsymbol{\eta}:=(\eta_{j})_{j\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}}\in\{-1,1\}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}
, define
g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+d_{Z}}\rightarrow(-1,1)
by

\displaystyle g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x,z):=\gamma_{n}\sum_{j\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}}\eta_{j}h_{n,j}(x,z).

To see that
g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in\mathcal{H}_{s}^{d}
, we first note that for any multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}
with
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq s
, we have

\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\infty}=\gamma_{n}\rho_{n}^{d/2+|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\|_{\infty}\leq c^{1/2}\max_{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}|\leq s}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}}h\|_{\infty}.

(S27)

Now fix
(x,z),(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}+d_{Z}}
; let
j\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}
denote the unique index such that
h_{n,j}(x,z)\neq 0
if it exists, and otherwise arbitrarily set
j=(1,\ldots,1)^{d}
. Similarly, let
j^{\prime}\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}
denote the unique index such that
h_{n,j^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})\neq 0
if it exists, and otherwise set
j^{\prime}=(1,\ldots,1)^{d}
. Then for any
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=\left\lceil s\right\rceil-1=:s_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x,z)-D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{n}\rho_{n}^{d/2+s_{0}}\bigl\{\bigl|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\bigl(\rho_{n}\cdot(x,z)-j+1\bigr)-D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\bigl(\rho_{n}\cdot(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})-j+1\bigr)\bigr|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\bigl|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\bigl(\rho_{n}\cdot(x,z)-j^{\prime}+1\bigr)-D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\bigl(\rho_{n}\cdot(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})-j^{\prime}+1\bigr)\bigr|\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{n}\rho_{n}^{d/2+s_{0}}\min\biggl(4\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\|_{\infty},2\max_{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}|=s_{0}+1}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}}h\|_{\infty}\rho_{n}\|(x,z)-(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})\|_{1}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{n}\rho_{n}^{d/2+s_{0}}\max\biggl(4\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\|_{\infty},2\max_{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}|\leq s_{0}+1}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}}h\|_{\infty}\biggr)\min\bigl(1,\rho_{n}\|(x,z)-(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})\|_{1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq c^{1/2}d^{1/2}\max\biggl(4\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\|_{\infty},2\max_{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}|\leq s_{0}+1}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\alpha}}}h\|_{\infty}\biggr)\|(x,z)-(x^{\prime},z^{\prime})\|^{s-s_{0}},

(S28)

where the final inequality uses the fact that
\min(1,t)^{y}\leq t^{y}
for any
t>0
and
y\in(0,1)
.
Using (
S27
) and (
S1.8
) and reducing
c>0
such that

c^{1/2}<\max\biggl(4\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\|_{\infty},2\max_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq s_{0}+1}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\|_{\infty},\max_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|\leq s}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}h\|_{\infty}\biggr)^{-1}\frac{C}{d^{1/2}},

if necessary, we ensure that
g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in\mathcal{H}_{s}^{d_{X}+d_{Z}}
with
\|g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}\leq C
.

Define now
Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}
such that
(X,Z)
is uniform on
[0,1]^{d_{X}}\times[0,1]^{d_{Z}}
and
Y
is Rademacher with

{\mathbb{E}}_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(Y\,|\,X=x,Z=z)=g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x,z).

Note that by construction

\displaystyle m_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(z):=\int_{[0,1]^{d_{X}}}g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x,z)\,\mathrm{d}x=0\quad\text{for any $\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\{-1,1\}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}$},

so
m_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in\mathcal{H}_{s}^{d_{Z}}
with
\|m_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}=0
. Further,

\displaystyle\tau_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}={\mathbb{E}}_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}\bigl[\bigl\{g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(X,Z)-m(Z)\bigr\}^{2}\bigr]=\gamma_{n}^{2}\rho_{n}^{d}\leq c^{2}n^{-\frac{4s}{4s+d}},

and we deduce from the definition of
\epsilon_{n}
that
Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\epsilon_{n})
for sufficiently large
n
. We let
\mathcal{Q}_{n}:=\bigl\{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}:\boldsymbol{\eta}\in\{-1,1\}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}\bigr\}
.

To see that (
S26
) is satisfied, we note that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{1}^{n},P_{0}^{n})

\displaystyle=-1+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|^{2}}\sum_{Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}q_{Q}^{\otimes n}q_{Q^{\prime}}^{\otimes n}\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}^{n}

\displaystyle=-1+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|^{2}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{A}}q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}\biggr)^{n}.

so it suffices to show that
\limsup
of the second term is at most
2
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. But

\displaystyle q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\{1+g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x,z)\}^{(1+y)/2}\{1-g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(x,z)\}^{(1-y)/2},

so, for
Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}},Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}}\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}\bigl(q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(X,1,Z)q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}}(X,1,Z)\,|\,Y=1\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}\bigl(q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}}(X,-1,Z)q_{Q_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}}(X,-1,Z)\,|\,Y=-1\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}\bigl(\bigl\{1+g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(X,Z)\bigr\}\bigl\{1+g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}(X,Z)\bigr\}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{2}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}\bigl(\bigl\{1-g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(X,Z)\bigr\}\bigl\{1-g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}(X,Z)\bigr\}\bigr)

\displaystyle=1+{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{0}}\bigl(g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}}(X,Z)g_{n,\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}(X,Z)\bigr)

\displaystyle=1+\gamma_{n}^{2}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}\in[\rho_{n}]^{d}}\eta_{j}\eta_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime}\int_{[0,1]^{d_{X}+d_{Z}}}h_{n,j}(x,z)h_{n,j^{\prime}}(x,z)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=1+\gamma_{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}.

Let
\boldsymbol{U}=(U_{1},\ldots,U_{\rho_{n}^{d}})
and
\boldsymbol{W}=(W_{1},\ldots,W_{\rho_{n}^{d}})
be independent random vectors, each with independent Rademacher components. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|^{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{A}}q_{Q}q_{Q^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}\biggr)^{n}=\frac{1}{2^{2\rho_{n}^{d}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}}(1+\gamma_{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime})^{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{2\rho_{n}^{d}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{\eta},\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}}e^{n\gamma_{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\eta}^{\prime}}=\mathbb{E}(e^{n\gamma_{n}^{2}\boldsymbol{U}^{\top}\boldsymbol{W}})=\prod_{j=1}^{\rho_{n}^{d}}\mathbb{E}(e^{n\gamma_{n}^{2}U_{j}W_{j}})

\displaystyle=\cosh(n\gamma_{n}^{2})^{\rho_{n}^{d}}\leq e^{n^{2}\gamma_{n}^{4}\rho_{n}^{d}/2}.

Thus,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Q}_{n}|^{2}}\sum_{Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}_{n}}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{A}}q_{Q}q_{Q^{\prime}}\,\mathrm{d}P_{0}\biggr)^{n}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}e^{n^{2}\gamma_{n}^{4}\rho_{n}^{d}/2}\leq\exp(c^{2}/2).

Taking
c\leq\sqrt{2\log 2}
, we have proved (
S26
) for
P_{0}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
, and the result follows.

Appendix S2
Auxiliary lemmas

S2.1
Uniform convergence results

Recall the ‘uniformly small in probability’ notation
o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
defined in Section
1.3
. As above, we sometimes omit the subscript
P
from quantities depending on
P
to simplify the presentation. In what follows we collect several technical lemmas that are used in the proofs in Section
S1
of the supplementary material.

Lemma S1
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real-valued random variables. Let
C>0
and suppose that
|X_{n}|\leq C
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Then
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|)=o(1)
.

Proof.

For any given
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle|X_{n}|=|X_{n}|\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{n}|>\epsilon\}}+|X_{n}|\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{n}|\leq\epsilon\}}\leq C\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{n}|>\epsilon\}}+\epsilon.

By the assumption that
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
, we can choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}|>\epsilon)<\epsilon/C
for
n\geq N
. It follows that for
n\geq N
,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|)\leq C\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>\epsilon)+\epsilon<2\epsilon.

Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, the result follows.
∎

The following lemma derives uniform stochastic boundedness of a sequence
(X_{n})
based on a conditional moment condition.

Lemma S2
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real-valued random variables on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
and let
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{F}
. For a positive sequence
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, possibly depending on
P
, suppose that
{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})=O_{\mathcal{P}}(a_{n})
. Then
X_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(a_{n})
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, given
\epsilon>0
, there exist
M_{\epsilon}>0
,
N_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
, both depending only on
\epsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\mathcal{A}_{n,P})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},

(S29)

where
\mathcal{A}_{n,P}:=\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\geq M_{\epsilon}a_{n}\bigr\}
. Then, by Markov’s inequality, for any
K_{\epsilon}>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|

\displaystyle\geq K_{\epsilon}a_{n})=\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(\frac{|X_{n}|}{a_{n}}\wedge K_{\epsilon}\geq K_{\epsilon}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{K_{\epsilon}}\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl(\frac{|X_{n}|}{a_{n}}\wedge K_{\epsilon}\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{(i)}}{\leq}\frac{1}{K_{\epsilon}}\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})}{a_{n}}\wedge K_{\epsilon}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{K_{\epsilon}}\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})}{a_{n}}\wedge K_{\epsilon}\biggr)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n,P}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{K_{\epsilon}}\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl\{\biggl(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})}{a_{n}}\wedge K_{\epsilon}\biggr)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{n,P}^{c}}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\mathcal{A}_{n,P})+\frac{M_{\epsilon}}{K_{\epsilon}}~\overset{\mathrm{(ii)}}{\leq}~\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{M_{\epsilon}}{K_{\epsilon}},

where step (i) uses conditional Jensen’s inequality and step (ii) uses the inequality (
S29
). Then the desired result follows by taking
K_{\epsilon}\geq 2M_{\epsilon}/\epsilon
.
∎

Lemma S3
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real-valued random variables. If
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and
Y_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
then
X_{n}Y_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. Then for any
M>0

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}Y_{n}|>\epsilon)\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>\epsilon/M)+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|Y_{n}|>M).

Choose
M>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough that
