\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|Y_{n}|>M)\leq\epsilon/2
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. By increasing
n_{0}
if necessary, we can ensure that
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>\epsilon/M)\leq\epsilon/2
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. The result follows.
∎

Lemma S4
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real-valued random variables. Suppose that
R_{n}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
X_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(R_{n})
and
R_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
. Then
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, for any
\epsilon>0
, there exist constants
M_{\epsilon}>0
and
N_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
, both depending only on
\epsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(R_{n}>\epsilon/M_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon/2\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>R_{n}M_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon/2.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(|X_{n}|>\epsilon,R_{n}>\epsilon/M_{\epsilon}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(|X_{n}|>\epsilon,R_{n}\leq\epsilon/M_{\epsilon}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(R_{n}>\epsilon/M_{\epsilon})+\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>R_{n}M_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon,

as required.
∎

Lemma S5
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences of real-valued random variables. For positive sequences
(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(b_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, suppose that
X_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(a_{n})
and
Y_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(b_{n})
. Then
X_{n}Y_{n}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(a_{n}b_{n})
.

Proof.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exist
N_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
and
M_{\epsilon},K_{\epsilon}>0
, all depending only on
\epsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>a_{n}M_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon/2\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|Y_{n}|>b_{n}K_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon/2.

Notice that if
|X_{n}Y_{n}|>a_{n}b_{n}M_{\epsilon}K_{\epsilon}
, then either
|X_{n}|>a_{n}M_{\epsilon}
or
|Y_{n}|>b_{n}K_{\epsilon}
. Therefore, by a union bound,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}Y_{n}|>a_{n}b_{n}M_{\epsilon}K_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|X_{n}|>a_{n}M_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|Y_{n}|>b_{n}K_{\epsilon})\leq\epsilon,

as desired.
∎

Lemma S6
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real-valued random variables on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
, and let
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{F}
. Suppose that
|X_{n}|=Y_{n}+Z_{n}
. If
Y_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and
{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Z_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
, then
X_{n}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1/2]
be given. By Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}|\geq\epsilon)

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}|\wedge\epsilon\geq\epsilon)\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n}|\wedge\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl((\epsilon^{2}+Z_{n})\wedge\epsilon\bigr)+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(|Y_{n}|>\epsilon^{2}).

The second term converges to
0
by assumption. For the first term, by Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl((\epsilon^{2}+Z_{n})\wedge\epsilon\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\Bigl[{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl((\epsilon^{2}+Z_{n})\wedge\epsilon\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\Bigl[\bigl\{\epsilon^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Z_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\bigr\}\wedge\epsilon\Bigr]

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(|{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Z_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})|>\epsilon^{2}\bigr).

The result therefore follows by our hypothesis on
{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Z_{n}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})
.
∎

Lemma S7
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of real-valued random variables and let
X
be another such variable. Assume that
|X_{n}-X|=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
and let
h:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function. Suppose that at least one of the following conditions hold:

(i)

h
is uniformly continuous,

(ii)

X
is uniformly tight, that is,

\lim_{M\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X|>M)=0.

Then
|h(X_{n})-h(X)|=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
be given. We need to show that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|h(X_{n})-h(X)|>\epsilon)=0.

If
h
is uniformly continuous, then we can find
\delta>0
such that
|h(x)-h(y)|\leq\epsilon
whenever
|x-y|\leq\delta
. Thus,

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|h(X_{n})-h(X)|>\epsilon)\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}-X|>\delta)\rightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
. On the other hand, suppose now that
X
is uniformly tight and let
M>0
. Since
h
is continuous, it is uniformly continuous on
[-M,M]
, so we can choose
\delta>0
such that
|h(x)-h(y)|\leq\epsilon
whenever
x,y\in[-M,M]
satisfy
|x-y|\leq\delta
. Hence, for
M>\delta
,

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|h(X_{n})-h(X)|>\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}-X|>\delta)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}|\vee|X|>M,|X_{n}-X|\leq\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X_{n}-X|>\delta)+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|X|>M-\delta)\rightarrow 0

as
n,M\to\infty
.
∎

Lemma S8
.

Let
(X_{n,i})_{n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n]}
be a triangular array of real-valued random variables and let
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a filtration on
\mathcal{F}
. Assume that

(i)

X_{n,1},\dots,X_{n,n}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(ii)

\mathbb{E}_{P}(X_{n,i}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n]
;

(iii)

\bigl|n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{P}(X_{n,i}^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})-1\bigr|=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
;

(iv)

there exists
\delta>0
such that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|X_{n,i}|^{2+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{\delta/2}).

Then
S_{n}:=n^{-1/2}\sum_{m=1}^{n}X_{n,m}
converges uniformly in distribution to
N(0,1)
, i.e.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}_{P}(S_{n}\leq x)-\Phi(x)|=0.

Proof.

We will make the dependence of
X_{n,m}
and
\mathcal{F}_{n}
on
P
clear by instead writing
X_{P,n,i}
and
\mathcal{F}_{P,n}
throughout. By
Kasy, 2019
it suffices to show that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{P_{n},n,i}\overset{d}{\to}N(0,1)

for any sequence
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathcal{P}
. Define the triangular array
W_{n,i}:=n^{-1/2}X_{P_{n},n,i}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[n]
, and let
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i}
be the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
\mathcal{F}_{P_{n},n}
that makes
X_{P_{n},n,1},\dots,X_{P_{n},n,i}
measurable (and
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,0}:=\mathcal{F}_{P_{n},n}
). We claim that
(W_{n,i},\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i})
form a martingale difference array. To see this, observe that
W_{n,i}
is
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i}
-measurable and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}(W_{n,i}\,|\,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}(X_{P_{n},n,i}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{P_{n},n},X_{P_{n},n,1},\dots,X_{P_{n},n,i-1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}(X_{P_{n},n,i}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{P_{n},n})=0,

where we have used assumptions (i) and (ii) in the penultimate and final equalities, respectively, and this establishes our claim. Now

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}(W_{n,i}^{2}\,|\,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i-1})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}(X_{P_{n},n,i}^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{P_{n},n})\overset{P}{\to}1,

by assumptions (i) and (iii), and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{2+\delta}\,|\,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i-1}\bigr)=\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}\bigl(|X_{P_{n},n,i}|^{2+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{P_{n},n}\bigr)\overset{P}{\to}0,

by assumptions (i) and (iv). It follows that for any
c>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|W_{n,i}|>c\}}\,|\,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{c^{\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P_{n}}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{2+\delta}\,|\,\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{n,i-1}\bigr)\overset{P}{\to}0,

so the conditional Lindeberg condition is satisfied. The result therefore follows by the Lindeberg–Feller central limit theorem for martingales
(e.g.
Durrett, 2019
, Theorem 8.2.4)
.
∎

Lemma S9
.

Let
(X_{n,i})_{n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n]}
be a triangular array of real-valued random variables and let
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a filtration on
\mathcal{F}
. Assume that

(i)

X_{n,1},\dots,X_{n,n}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

(ii)

there exists
\delta\in(0,1]
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|X_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{1+\delta}).

Then
S_{n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{n,i}
and
\mu_{P,n}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(X_{n,i}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})
satisfy
|S_{n}-\mu_{P,n}|=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
; i.e., for any
\epsilon>0

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}(|S_{n}-\mu_{P,n}|>\epsilon)=0.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in[n]
, define
W_{n,i}:=X_{n,i}-\mu_{P,n}
. Note that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\delta}\biggl(\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|X_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)+n|\mu_{P,n}|^{1+\delta}\biggr)

(S30)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\delta+1}\biggl(\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|X_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{1+\delta}),

by assumption (ii). We need to show that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{n,i}\biggr|\geq\epsilon\biggr)=0.

Define
W_{n,i}^{<}:=W_{n,i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|W_{n,i}|\leq n\}}
and
W_{n,i}^{>}:=W_{n,i}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|W_{n,i}|>n\}}
. By the triangle inequality we can write

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(

\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{n,i}\biggr|\geq\epsilon\biggr)\leq\underbrace{\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[W_{n,i}^{<}-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})]\biggr|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)}_{\textup{I}_{n}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{n,i}^{>}\biggr|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)}_{\textup{II}_{n}}+\underbrace{\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\biggr|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)}_{\textup{III}_{n}},

and we will treat each term separately. Considering first
\textup{I}_{n}
, we note that

\displaystyle\textup{I}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[W_{n,i}^{<}-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})]\biggr|\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{\epsilon^{2}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl(\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl[W_{n,i}^{<}-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\bigr]\biggr\}^{2}\wedge\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{9}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{\epsilon^{2}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl({\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl[\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}[W_{n,i}^{<}-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})]\biggr\}^{2}\biggm|\mathcal{F}_{n}\biggr]\wedge\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{9}\biggr),

where we have applied Markov’s inequality and the tower property combined with the monotonicity of conditional expectations to move the minimum inside the conditional expectation. By assumption (i), the terms in the sum of squares are conditionally independent, so the cross terms vanish, and we find

\displaystyle\textup{I}_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{\epsilon^{2}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl(\biggl\{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathrm{Var}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\biggr\}\wedge\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{9}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{\epsilon^{2}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl\{(W_{n,i}^{<})^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr\}\wedge\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{9}\biggr).

Now, for
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl\{

\displaystyle(W_{n,i}^{<})^{2}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr\}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{P}\bigl((W_{n,i}^{<})^{2}>t\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}2y\,\mathbb{P}_{P}(|W_{n,i}^{<}|>y\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{n}2y\,\mathbb{P}_{P}(|W_{n,i}|>y\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\,\mathrm{d}y=n^{2}\int_{0}^{1}2u\,\mathbb{P}_{P}(|W_{n,i}|>nu\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq n^{1-\delta}\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}2u^{-\delta}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr){\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|W_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})=\frac{2}{1-\delta}n^{1-\delta}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|W_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}),

where we have used the substitutions
y=\sqrt{t}
and
u=(1/n)y
, as well as the conditional version of Markov’s inequality. We deduce that for any
\delta\in(0,1]
,

\textup{I}_{n}\leq\frac{9}{\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(\frac{2\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta\in(0,1)\}}}{1-\delta}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{\delta=1\}}\biggr)\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl\{(|W_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr\}\wedge\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{9}\biggr)\rightarrow 0,

by (
S30
) and Lemma
S1
.

We now deal with
\textup{II}_{n}
and
\textup{III}_{n}
by first noting that, using similar
\epsilon/3
-thresholding as above, we obtain

\displaystyle\textup{III}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggl(\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\biggr|\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)

by Markov’s inequality. Now, by construction, we can write

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{<}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{>}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}),

and thus by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\textup{III}_{n}\leq\frac{3}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggl(\biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl|{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(W_{n,i}^{>}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n})\bigr|\biggr]\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)+\frac{3}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\Bigl(|R_{n}|\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\Bigr).

The second term converges to
0
by Lemma
S1
, so it remains to show that the first term converges to
0
. Now
\textup{II}_{n}
can be seen to also be upper bounded by the first term by a similar argument to the one given above, so we are done if we can show that the first term converges to
0
.
Applying conditional Hölder’s inequality
(
Gut, 2013
, Theorem 10.1.6)
followed by conditional Markov’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\frac{3}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggl(\biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|W_{n,i}^{>}|\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)\biggr]\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggl(\biggl[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{1+\delta}}\mathbb{P}_{P}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|>n\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)^{\frac{\delta}{1+\delta}}\biggr]\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{\epsilon}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}_{P}\biggl(\biggl[\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(|W_{n,i}|^{1+\delta}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)\biggr]\wedge\frac{\epsilon}{3}\biggr).

Finally, combining the above with (
S30
) and Lemma
S1
yields the desired result.
∎

Lemma S10
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
denote a family of distributions of
(Y,Z)
taking values in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Define
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\equiv\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}(ZZ^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and suppose that this is invertible for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Let
\boldsymbol{\beta}\equiv\boldsymbol{\beta}_{P}:=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(ZY)
,
\varepsilon\equiv\varepsilon_{P}:=Y-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{P}^{\top}Z
and
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\equiv\boldsymbol{\Theta}_{P}:={\mathbb{E}}(ZZ^{\top}\varepsilon^{2})
. Suppose there exist
C,c,\delta>0
such that the following hold:

(i)

\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\min\{\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}),\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})\}\geq c

(ii)

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\max\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\|Z\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2+\delta}),{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\|Z\|_{\infty}^{2+\delta})\bigr\}\leq C
.

Given independent copies
(Y_{1},Z_{1}),\dots,(Y_{n},Z_{n})
of
(Y,Z)
, let
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}
denote the ordinary least squares estimator from regressing
Y
on
Z
. Then,

\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta})

converges uniformly to a standard
d
-variate Gaussian distribution.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{d}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}
and write
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jk}
for the
(j,k)
th entry of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
. We first argue that

\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(S31)

By the equivalence of finite-dimensional norms, it suffices to show that
\max_{j,k\in[d]}|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{jk}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jk}|=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
, which is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{jk}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jk}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1)
for all
j,k\in[d]
. To show this final claim, let
Z_{ij}
denote the
j
th component of
Z_{i}
. Then

\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}_{jk}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{jk}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl\{Z_{ij}Z_{ik}-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(Z_{ij}Z_{ik}\bigr)\bigr\}.

Fix
j,k\in[d]
and define the triangular array
X_{n,i}:=Z_{ij}Z_{ik}-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Z_{ij}Z_{ik})
for
i\in[n]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We aim to apply Lemma
S9
to
(X_{n,i})_{n\in\mathbb{N},i\in[n]}
, where we condition on the trivial
\sigma
-algebra and have
\mu_{P,n}=0
. Now, condition (i) of Lemma
S9
is satisfied by hypothesis, and for condition (ii) we have by Jensen’s inequality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|X_{n,i}|^{1+\delta/2})\leq 2^{1+\delta/2}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(|Z_{1j}Z_{1k}|^{1+\delta/2})\leq 2^{1+\delta/2}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\|Z\|_{\infty}^{2+\delta})\leq 2^{1+\delta/2}C.

Thus (
S31
) follows.

We now argue that

\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(S32)

It follows immediately our assumption on
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
by Lemma
S16
that for any
\epsilon>0
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\epsilon)\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>c/2)+{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq c^{2}\epsilon/2).

Thus taking suprema over
\mathcal{P}
and applying (
S31
), we have shown (
S32
).

We now turn to proving the stated result. Defining
U_{n}:=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{-1/2}Z_{i}\varepsilon_{i}
, we have

\sqrt{n}\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\widehat{\beta}}-\boldsymbol{\beta})=\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{-1/2}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{1/2}U_{n}.

The summands in the definition of
U_{n}
are mean zero with identity covariance matrix and they satisfy Lyapunov’s condition since

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\|\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{-1/2}Z\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2+\delta}\bigr)\leq\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})^{-(1+\delta/2)}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\|Z\varepsilon\|_{2}^{2+\delta}\bigr)\leq c^{-(1+\delta/2)}C.

The Lindeberg–Feller central limit theorem
(
van der Vaart, 1998
, Proposition 2.27)
therefore yields that
U_{n}
converges uniformly to a
d
-variate standard Gaussian. Combining this with a uniform version of Slutsky’s theorem
(
Bengs and Holzmann, 2019
, Theorem 6.3)
and (
S32
) yields the desired result.
∎

S2.2
Miscellaneous results

Proposition S11
.

Let
X,Y,Z
be random variables with
Y\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathbb{E}}(Y^{4})<\infty
and
{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)>0
almost surely. Then

\frac{\left({\mathbb{E}}[\{Y-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)\}f(X,Z)]\right)^{2}}{{\mathbb{E}}[\{Y-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)\}^{2}f(X,Z)^{2}]}

(S33)

is maximised over
f
with
0<{\mathbb{E}}\{f(X,Z)^{4}\}<\infty
by

f(X,Z)\propto\frac{{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)}{{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)},

and up to positive scaling this is almost surely the unique maximiser.

Proof.

The denominator of (
S33
) may be written as
{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)f(X,Z)^{2}\}
. Turning to the numerator, we have by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle\bigl({\mathbb{E}}[\{Y-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)\}

\displaystyle f(X,Z)]\bigr)^{2}=\Bigl({\mathbb{E}}[\{{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)\}f(X,Z)]\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\{{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)f(X,Z)^{2}\}\,\,{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\frac{\{{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)\}^{2}}{{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)}\biggr].

(S34)

Since the first factor in this final expression cancels with the denominator of (
S33
), and since we have equality in (
S2.2
) if and only if
f(X,Z)\propto\{{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)-{\mathbb{E}}(Y\,|\,Z)\}/{\mathrm{Var}}(Y\,|\,X,Z)
almost surely, the result follows.
∎

Proposition S12
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{B}
be arbitrarily dependent standard normal random variables. For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
B\in\mathbb{N}
,

{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}Z_{b}\geq\frac{\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}{\alpha}\biggr)\leq\alpha,

where
\varphi
denotes the density function of
N(0,1)
. Moreover,

\lim_{\alpha\rightarrow 0}\frac{\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}{z_{1-\alpha}}=1.

Proof.

Denoting
\bar{Z}:=B^{-1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}Z_{b}
, observe that, for any
t,a\in\mathbb{R}
with
t>a
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(\bar{Z}\geq t)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\bigl(\max\{\bar{Z}-a,0\}\geq t-a\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\max\{\bar{Z}-a,0\}\bigr)}{t-a}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\max\{Z_{1}-a,0\}\bigr)}{t-a},

where the first inequality holds by Markov’s inequality and the second by Jensen’s inequality. Using the identity
\max\{x,0\}=(x+|x|)/2
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
, as well as the mean formula for a folded normal random variable, we compute the expectation above as

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\max\{Z_{1}-a,0\}\bigr)=\varphi(a)+a\Phi(a)-a.

Therefore, taking
t:=\alpha^{-1}\{\varphi(a)+a\Phi(a)-a\}+a>a
, we have

{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\bar{Z}\geq\frac{\varphi(a)+a\Phi(a)-(1-\alpha)a}{\alpha}\biggr)\leq\alpha.

Now the first claim follows by observing that

\min_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\bigl\{\varphi(a)+a\Phi(a)-(1-\alpha)a\bigr\}=\varphi(z_{1-\alpha}).

For the second claim, we invoke the Mills ratio bounds

\frac{z}{z^{2}+1}<\frac{1-\Phi(z)}{\varphi(z)}<\frac{1}{z}

for
z>0
. Applying this bound at
z=z_{1-\alpha}
yields

1<\frac{\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}{z_{1-\alpha}}<1+\frac{1}{z_{1-\alpha}^{2}},

so the result follows.
∎

Corollary S13
.

Let
(X_{P,n}^{b})_{n\in\mathbb{N},b\in[B]}
be sequences of real-valued random variables whose distributions are determined by
P\in\mathcal{P}
. Suppose that for each
b\in[B]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|{\mathbb{P}}_{P}(X_{P,n}^{b}\leq x)-\Phi(x)|=0.

(S35)

Then for any fixed value of
\alpha\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}X_{P,n}^{b}\geq\frac{\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}{\alpha}\biggr)\leq\alpha.

Proof.

We aim to prove that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
N_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(\bar{X}_{P,n}\geq\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})\bigr)\leq\alpha+\epsilon,

(S36)

with
\bar{X}_{P,n}:=B^{-1}\sum_{b=1}^{B}X_{P,n}^{b}
. Given
\epsilon>0
, the asymptotic normality condition (
S35
) ensures the existence of
K_{\epsilon}>0
and
N_{\epsilon,1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{P,n,\epsilon}:=\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon},\ldots,X_{P,n}^{B}-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}
satisfies

\displaystyle\inf_{n\geq N_{\epsilon,1}}\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{P,n,\epsilon}\bigr)\geq 1-\epsilon/2.

Then following the proof of Proposition
S12
with
t=\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})+z_{1-\alpha}
and
a=z_{1-\alpha}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon,1}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(\bar{X}_{n,P}\geq\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon,1}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\max\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha},0\}\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}}\bigr)}{\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}+\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon,1}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\bigl(\mathcal{A}_{P,n,\epsilon}^{c}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon,1}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\max\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha},0\}\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}}\bigr)}{\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Observe that
f:x\mapsto\max\{x-z_{1-\alpha},0\}\mathbbm{1}_{\{x-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}}
is a bounded, upper semi-continuous function. Therefore,
Bengs and Holzmann, 2019
along with the asymptotic normality condition (
S35
) guarantees the existence of
N_{\epsilon,2}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq N_{\epsilon,2}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\max\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha},0\}\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{P,n}^{1}-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}}\bigr)}{\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\max\{Z-z_{1-\alpha},0\}\mathbbm{1}_{\{Z-z_{1-\alpha}\leq K_{\epsilon}\}}\bigr)}{\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq\alpha+\frac{\epsilon}{2},

where
Z\sim N(0,1)
. The claim (
S36
) therefore follows with
N_{\epsilon}:=\max\{N_{\epsilon,1},N_{\epsilon,2}\}
.
∎

We can further develop a more refined threshold than the one obtained in Proposition
S12
. For
Z\sim N(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
and
B\geq 2
, define

\displaystyle H_{\alpha}(x):=(B-1)\Phi^{-1}\bigl(1-\alpha+(B-1)x\bigr)+\Phi^{-1}(1-x)\quad\text{for $x\in[0,\alpha/B]$,}

and

\displaystyle c_{B}(\alpha):=\min\biggl\{c\in\bigl[0,\alpha/B\bigr]:\int_{c}^{\frac{\alpha}{B}}H_{\alpha}(t)\mathrm{d}t\geq\biggl(\frac{\alpha}{B}-c\biggr)H_{\alpha}(c)\biggr\}.

Write

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}:=\begin{cases}B^{-1}H_{\alpha}\bigl(c_{B}(\alpha)\bigr)\quad&\text{if $c_{B}(\alpha)>0$,}\\[5.0pt]
\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})\quad&\text{if $c_{B}(\alpha)=0$.}\end{cases}

Having this notation in place,
Wang et al., 2013
yields

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}Z_{b}>q_{1-\alpha,B}\biggr)\leq\alpha.

Notably, according to
Wang et al., 2013
, this threshold is sharp in the sense that

\displaystyle q_{1-\alpha,B}=\sup_{Z_{b}\sim N(0,1),b\in[B]}\inf\biggl\{s\in\mathbb{R}:{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\frac{1}{B}\sum_{b=1}^{B}Z_{b}\leq s\biggr)\geq 1-\alpha\biggr\}.

It is also worth noting that we have
q_{1-\alpha,2}=z_{1-\alpha/2}
.
Moreover,
\lim_{B\rightarrow\infty}q_{1-\alpha,B}=\alpha^{-1}\varphi(z_{1-\alpha})
, which illustrates that Proposition
S12
is not improvable for large
B
.

Proposition S14
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
4
, and suppose that Assumption
3
holds.

(i)

If
Y\mbox{${}\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp{}$}X\,|\,Z
, then (
S2
) is satisfied.

(ii)

If
\widehat{m}
is formed using a sample independent from
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then (
S2
) is satisfied.

(iii)

If
\widehat{m}
is a linear smoother, then (
S2
) is satisfied.

Proof.

As in the main proofs in Section
S1
of the supplementary material, we suppress dependence on
P
in what follows.

(i) Under conditional independence,
R_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
, so (
S2
) is satisfied.

(ii) Define
w(z_{1},z_{2}):={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{\widehat{m}(z_{1})-m(z_{1})\}\{\widehat{m}(z_{2})-m(z_{2})\}\bigr)
and
M_{i}:=m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})
. Note that since
(X_{i},Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{${}\perp\mkern-11.0mu\perp{}$}\widehat{m}
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)=w(Z_{i},Z_{j})={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(M_{i}M_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)

by, e.g.,
Durrett, 2019
. Thus
R_{ij}=0
for
i\neq j
and (
S2
) is satisfied.

(iii) It suffices to show that
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(R_{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr)=0
for
i\neq j
. Note that when
\widehat{m}(\cdot)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(Z_{k},\cdot)Y_{k}
is a linear smoother,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(Z_{k},Z_{i}){\mathbb{E}}\bigl(Y_{k}\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(Z_{k},Z_{i}){\mathbb{E}}\bigl(Y_{k}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr).

Based on this identity, it can be seen that

\displaystyle R_{ij}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\widehat{m}(Z_{j})\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr\}-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl\{\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\widehat{m}(Z_{j})\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(Z_{k},Z_{i})\omega(Z_{k},Z_{j})\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{1\leq k\neq k^{\prime}\leq n}\omega(Z_{k},Z_{i})\omega(Z_{k^{\prime}},Z_{j})\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(Y_{k}Y_{k^{\prime}}\,|\,(X_{i^{\prime}},Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)-{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(Y_{k}Y_{k^{\prime}}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n}\bigr)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(Z_{k},Z_{i})\omega(Z_{k},Z_{j})\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,X_{k},Z_{k})-{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,Z_{k})\bigr\}.

Thus

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(R_{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(Z_{k},Z_{i})\omega(Z_{k},Z_{j}){\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,X_{k},Z_{k})-{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,Z_{k})\bigr\}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\Bigr].

When
i\neq j
at least one of
i
and
j
differs from
k\in[n]
. Without loss of generality, assume
k\neq i
, so that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\{

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,X_{k},Z_{k})-{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,Z_{k})\bigr\}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=~

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Bigl[\bigl\{{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,X_{k},Z_{k})-{\mathbb{E}}(Y_{k}^{2}\,|\,Z_{k})\bigr\}\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}(\xi_{i}\,|\,Z_{i},\widehat{f})}_{=0}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\Bigr]=0.

Therefore, we conclude that
{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(R_{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,|\,(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\widehat{f}\bigr)=0
when
\widehat{m}
is a linear smoother and thus (
S2
) is satisfied.
∎

Proposition
S15
below seeks to control an average product error between population residuals
\xi_{i}
and regression errors
\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-m_{P}(Z_{i})
. To obtain the desired
o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1/2})
conclusion, a naive application of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality would require stronger assumptions on the performance of our regression estimate than would be reasonable. Sample splitting provides one way of restoring independence and allows the conclusion to hold, but the purpose of this result is to show that sample splitting is not needed provided that our regression estimate is
sufficiently stable
in the sense that conditions (ii) and (iii) below hold (in addition to other standard conditions that would be required for the conclusion under sample splitting).

Proposition S15
.

Let
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of
\sigma
-algebras and let
(Y_{i},Z_{i},\xi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
be a sequence of random variables taking values in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathbb{R}
that are independent and identically distributed conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Let
\widehat{m}
denote an estimate of the regression function
m_{P}:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
m_{P}(z):={\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Y\,|\,Z=z)
formed by regressing
(Y_{i})_{i\in[n]}
on
(Z_{i})_{i\in[n]}
. For indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in[n]
, we define
\widehat{m}^{-i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}
to be the estimate formed by regressing
Y
on
Z
with the same algorithm as for
\widehat{m}
except we do not use the observations with indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{P}
is a class of distributions for which

(i)

\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(m_{P}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(ii)

\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

(iii)

\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

(iv)

For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in[n]
we have
{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\xi_{i}\,|\,Z_{i},\mathcal{F}_{n})=0
for all
P\in\mathcal{P}
and there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\xi_{i}^{2})\leq C
.

Then

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\bigl(m_{P}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

Proof.

As in the main proofs in Section
S1
, we suppress dependence on
P
in what follows. Assumption (
iii
) implies that

\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n-1}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,n}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-n}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\leq\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

which, by re-indexing implies,

\frac{1}{n}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

(S37)

By Markov’s inequality and (
iv
) we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(1).

(S38)

To prove the desired result, we write

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr).

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, (
S38
) and (
S37
), we have

\displaystyle\biggl|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1/2}).

To deal with the first term in the initial decomposition, we write

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 7.11317pt+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr).

For the first term, we note that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\leq\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl\{\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}+\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}+2\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1})

by (
S38
), (
i
) and (
S37
). For the second term, we write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}

\displaystyle\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{I}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{II}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{III}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}(Z_{j}))}_{\textup{IV}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{V}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{VI}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{VII}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr)}_{\textup{VIII}}.

Using the fact that the triples
(Y_{i},Z_{i},\xi_{i})_{i\in[n]}
are conditionally independent given
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, together with (
iv
), for each of the summands in
I
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{j})\bigr)\biggm|(Y_{i^{\prime}})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i^{\prime}=1\\
i^{\prime}\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n},(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\xi_{j},\mathcal{F}_{n}\biggr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\xi_{i}\biggm|(Y_{i^{\prime}})_{\begin{subarray}{c}i^{\prime}=1\\
i^{\prime}\neq i\end{subarray}}^{n},(Z_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}=1}^{n},\xi_{j},\mathcal{F}_{n}\biggr)\xi_{j}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{j})\bigr)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}(\xi_{i}\,|\,Z_{i},\mathcal{F}_{n})\xi_{j}\bigl(m(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})\bigr)\bigl(m(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{j})\bigr)=0.

Thus,

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\textup{I}\,|\,(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)=0.

We can apply a similar argument to the summands of
II
and thus also conclude that

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\textup{II}\,|\,(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{n},\mathcal{F}_{n}\bigr)=0.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, (
S38
) and (
iii
), we have

\displaystyle|\textup{III}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

By Cauchy–Schwarz, (
S38
) and (
ii
), we have

\displaystyle|\textup{IV}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\max_{j\in[n]}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=1\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

By Cauchy–Schwarz, (
S38
) and (
S37
), we have

\displaystyle|\textup{V}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

By Cauchy–Schwarz and the bounds above we have

\displaystyle|\textup{VI}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}(Z_{j})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i}))^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle|\textup{VII}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-i,j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1})

and

\displaystyle|\textup{VIII}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{i}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}(Z_{j})-\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{j})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}^{n}\xi_{j}^{2}\bigl(\widehat{m}^{-j}(Z_{i})-\widehat{m}(Z_{i})\bigr)^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=o_{\mathcal{P}}(n^{-1}).

Combining the above with Lemma
S6
yields the desired result.
∎

Lemma S16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
be symmetric matrices and suppose that
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{A})\geq c
and
\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq c/2
for some
c>0
. Then
\boldsymbol{B}
is invertible and

\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2c^{-2}\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Proof.

By Weyl’s inequality and our assumptions, we have

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{B})\geq\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{A})-\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq c/2,

so
\boldsymbol{B}
is invertible. We can therefore write

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle=\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{B}-\boldsymbol{A})\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Moreover since
\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1/2
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{B}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}}{1-\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\boldsymbol{A}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq 2c^{-2}\|\boldsymbol{A}-\boldsymbol{B}\|_{\mathrm{op}},

as required.
∎

Lemma S17
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
6
and assume that
X
and
Z
are independent for all
P\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
. Then (
14
) is satisfied for sufficiently small
c>0
.

Proof.

Recalling the definitions of
V
and
\boldsymbol{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{X}}
from Proposition
S31
, we note that for every
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{Z}}
, we can define
\boldsymbol{w}:=\boldsymbol{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{v}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{K_{Z}}\otimes_{\mathrm{Kron}}\boldsymbol{1})\boldsymbol{v}\in V
so that

\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)=\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\phi}^{Z}(Z),

by Proposition
S31
. Therefore

\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P}\boldsymbol{w}={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl({\mathrm{Var}}_{P}(\boldsymbol{v}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\phi}^{Z}(Z)\,|\,Z)\bigr)=0.

Since
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{XZ}}:\boldsymbol{\Pi}x=x\}=V^{\perp}
is
(K_{XZ}-K_{Z})
-dimensional and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P}
is non-negative definite, we conclude that

\widetilde{\lambda}_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P})=\lambda_{K_{XZ}-K_{Z}-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P}),

where
\lambda_{k}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P})
denotes the
k
th largest eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P}
.
We can write

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P}=\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)^{\top}\bigr)}_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}}-\underbrace{{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl({\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)\,|\,Z){\mathbb{E}}(\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)\,|\,Z)^{\top}\bigr)}_{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{P}}.

Denote the Kronecker product by
\otimes_{\mathrm{Kron}}
and note that for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{X}}
and
\boldsymbol{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{Z}}
, we have

\boldsymbol{x}\otimes\boldsymbol{z}=(\boldsymbol{I}_{K_{Z}}\otimes_{\mathrm{Kron}}\boldsymbol{x})\boldsymbol{z}.

Write
\boldsymbol{A}_{P}:=\boldsymbol{I}_{K_{Z}}\otimes_{\mathrm{Kron}}{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{X}(X)\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{X}K_{Z}\times K_{Z}}
. Then, since
X
and
Z
are independent,

{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}(X,Z)\,|\,Z\bigr)={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{X}(X)\bigr)\otimes\boldsymbol{\phi}^{Z}(Z)=\boldsymbol{A}_{P}\boldsymbol{\phi}^{Z}(Z).

Defining
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{Z,P}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}^{Z}(Z)\boldsymbol{\phi}^{Z}(Z)^{\top}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{K_{Z}\times K_{Z}}
, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{P}=\boldsymbol{A}_{P}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{Z,P}\boldsymbol{A}_{P}^{\top}
, so we deduce that
\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{P})\leq\mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{Z,P})\leq K_{Z}
. Hence, by Weyl’s inequality,

\displaystyle\lambda_{K_{XZ}-K_{Z}-1}(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{P})\geq\lambda_{K_{XZ}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})+\lambda_{K_{XZ}-K_{Z}-1}(-\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{P})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\lambda_{K_{XZ}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})-\lambda_{K_{Z}+1}(\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{P})=\lambda_{K_{XZ}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})\geq c_{s}(r)^{d}K_{XZ}^{-1}\inf_{(x,z)\in[0,1]^{d}}p_{P}(x,z),

by Proposition
S23
(d). This proves the desired claim.
∎

Corollary S18
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
S37
. Assume that
\beta=\lambda n^{-1/2}
and denote
r:=n_{2}/n_{1}
. Then, given any
\delta>0
, we can choose
\lambda_{0}\equiv\lambda_{0}(\alpha,\delta,\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi})>0
and
r_{0}\equiv r_{0}(\lambda_{0},\delta,\sigma_{\beta})>0
such that

\psi<\frac{1}{2}+\delta,

for all
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
and
r\in(0,r_{0}]
. Further, given
\delta>0
, we can choose
\lambda_{1}\equiv\lambda_{1}(\alpha,\delta,\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi})>0
and
r_{1}\equiv r_{1}(\lambda_{1},\delta,\sigma_{\beta})>0
such that

\psi<\alpha+\delta

for all
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{1}]
and
r\geq r_{1}
.

Proof.

To prove the first claim, note that, for
r<1/2
,

\psi\leq\Phi\biggl(\frac{\lambda r^{1/2}}{\sigma_{\beta}}\biggr)+\Phi\biggl(z_{\alpha}-\frac{\lambda\sigma_{\xi}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi}}\biggr).

We can now choose
\lambda_{0}\equiv\lambda_{0}(\alpha,\delta,\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi})>0
large enough that the second term is at most
\delta/2
for
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
and then choose
r_{0}\equiv r_{0}(\lambda_{0},\delta,\sigma_{\beta})>0
small enough that the first term is less than
1/2+\delta/2
for
r\in(0,r_{0}]
.

To prove the second claim, note that

\psi\leq\Phi\biggl(z_{\alpha}+\frac{\lambda\sigma_{\xi}^{2}}{\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi}}\biggr)+\Phi\biggl(-\frac{\lambda r^{1/2}}{\sigma_{\beta}}\biggr).

We can now choose
\lambda_{1}\equiv\lambda_{1}(\alpha,\delta,\sigma_{\xi},\sigma_{\varepsilon\xi})>0
small enough that the first term is at most
\delta/2+\alpha
for
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{1}]
and then choose
r_{1}\equiv r_{1}(\lambda_{1},\delta,\sigma_{\beta})>0
large enough that the second term is less than
\delta/2
for
r\geq r_{1}
.
∎

Appendix S3
A discussion of the test of
Williamson et al., 2023

Like our proposal, the test proposed by
Williamson et al., 2023
relies on sample splitting. However, their test suffers from two issues as we describe below. To this end, we start by formalising their testing procedure. First split the data
\mathcal{D}=\{(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})\}_{i=1}^{2n}
randomly into
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
both of size
n
and let
\mathcal{I}_{1}
and
\mathcal{I}_{2}
denote the corresponding data indices. We write the sample mean of
Y
for each split as
\overline{Y}_{1}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}Y_{i}
and
\overline{Y}_{2}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}Y_{i}
respectively. Recall the definitions of
g
and
m
from Section
2.1
and let
\widehat{g}
and
\widehat{m}
denote generic estimators of these, where
\widehat{g}
is constructed on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\widehat{m}
is constructed on
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. For notational convenience, we define
\mu_{0}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Y)
,
\sigma_{Y}^{2}:={\mathrm{Var}}_{P}(Y)
,
\tau_{xz,0}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl\{\bigl(g(X,Z)-\mu_{0}\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}
and
\tau_{z,0}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl\{\bigl(m(Z)-\mu_{0}\bigr)^{2}\bigr\}
. Let us further define

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{1})^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\{Y_{i}-\widehat{g}(X_{i},Z_{i})\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{1})^{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(Y_{i}-\widehat{m}(Z_{i}))^{2}}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{2})^{2}},

and denote their population counterparts by

\displaystyle v_{1}:=\frac{\tau_{xz,0}}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad v_{2}:=\frac{\tau_{z,0}}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}.

As shown in
Williamson et al., 2021
, the influence functions of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are given by

\varphi_{1}(x,y,z):=\frac{2\{y-g(x,z)\}\{g(x,z)-\mu_{0}\}+\{g(x,z)-\mu_{0}\}^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}-\tau_{xz,0}\biggl\{\frac{y-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Y)}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}\biggr\}^{2}

and

\varphi_{2}(y,z):=\frac{2\{y-m(z)\}\{m(z)-\mu_{0}\}+\{m(z)-\mu_{0}\}^{2}}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}-\tau_{z,0}\biggl\{\frac{y-{\mathbb{E}}_{P}(Y)}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}}\biggr\}^{2}

respectively. Finally, by letting
\widehat{\eta}_{1}
and
\widehat{\eta}_{2}
be consistent estimators of
\eta_{1}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\varphi_{1}(X,Y,Z)^{2})
and
\eta_{2}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}(\varphi_{2}(Y,Z)^{2})
, the test statistic proposed by
Williamson et al., 2023
is given as

\displaystyle T_{\mathrm{W}}:=\frac{\widehat{v}_{1}-\widehat{v}_{2}}{\sqrt{n^{-1}(\widehat{\eta}_{1}+\widehat{\eta}_{2})}}.

The test statistic
T_{\mathrm{W}}
is calibrated based on a normal approximation and the null of
\tau_{P}=0
is rejected if
T_{\mathrm{W}}>z_{1-\alpha}
. Having specified the test function, we are now ready to describe the issues mentioned above.

S3.1
Lack of power

We shall see that the
Williamson et al., 2023
test has the asymptotic power equal to its size whenever
\sqrt{n}\tau_{P}\rightarrow 0
, under some regularity conditions. This property is true even for the simple linear model where the optimal detection boundary is known to be
\tau_{P}\asymp n^{-1}
. To see this, suppose that the assumptions of
Williamson et al., 2021
are satisfied for
\widehat{v}_{1}
and
\widehat{v}_{2}
. That is,
\widehat{v}_{1}
and
\widehat{v}_{2}
are asymptotically linear with influence functions
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
, respectively, so that

\widehat{v}_{1}-v_{1}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}\varphi_{1}(X_{i},Y_{i},Z_{i})+o_{P}\bigl(n^{-1/2}\bigr)

and

\widehat{v}_{2}-v_{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}\varphi_{2}(Y_{i},Z_{i})+o_{P}\bigl(n^{-1/2}\bigr).

The asymptotic validity of the approach of
Williamson et al., 2023
comes from the fact that the individual influence functions
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are not necessarily degenerate under the null. In particular, when
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are non-zero, the central limit theorem guarantees that
T_{\mathrm{W}}
converges in distribution to
N(0,1)
under the null (where
v_{1}=v_{2}
). Similarly, we can also establish the asymptotic normality of
T_{\mathrm{W}}
under the alternative in the case where
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are non-zero. This asymptotic normality allows us to describe the asymptotic power expression of the given test. More formally, the central limit theorem yields

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\widehat{v}_{1}-v_{1})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N(0,\eta_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\sqrt{n}(\widehat{v}_{2}-v_{2})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N(0,\eta_{2}).

Hence, by Slutsky’s theorem and the independence of
\widehat{v}_{1}
and
\widehat{v}_{2}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{(\widehat{v}_{1}-\widehat{v}_{2})-(v_{1}-v_{2})}{\sqrt{n^{-1}(\widehat{\eta}_{1}+\widehat{\eta}_{2})}}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}N(0,1).

This shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P}(T_{\mathrm{W}}>z_{1-\alpha})\rightarrow\Phi\biggl(z_{\alpha}+\frac{\sqrt{n}\tau_{P}}{\sigma_{Y}^{2}\sqrt{\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}}\biggr),

where we have used the fact that
v_{1}-v_{2}=\tau_{P}/\sigma_{Y}^{2}
. Therefore, when
\sigma_{Y}^{2},\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are strictly bounded below by some positive constant, the power converges to the nominal level
\alpha
whenever
\sqrt{n}\tau_{P}\rightarrow 0
.

S3.2
Asymptotic validity

The previous argument hinges on the condition that
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are non-zero. As acknowledged by
Williamson et al., 2023
, the asymptotic validity of their test is no longer guaranteed when
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
are zero. We illustrate this by considering a specific example.

Consider a simple linear model where
Y=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}X+\beta_{2}Z+\varepsilon
and
(X,Z,\varepsilon)^{\top}
follows a multivariate normal distribution with zero mean and identity covariance matrix. Assume that
\beta_{1}=\beta_{2}=0
. In this scenario,
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are the zero functions on their respective domains, so
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=0
. Letting
(\widehat{\beta}_{0},\widehat{\beta}_{1},\widehat{\beta}_{1})
denote the least squares estimator of
(\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
based on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and letting
F_{k_{1},k_{2}}
denote the
F
-distribution with
(k_{1},k_{2})
degrees of freedom, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{1}=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{1})^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}(Y_{i}-\widehat{\beta}_{0}-\widehat{\beta}_{1}X_{i}-\widehat{\beta}_{2}Z_{i})^{2}}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{1}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{1})^{2}}\sim\frac{2}{n-1}F_{2,n-1}.

Similarly, writing
(\widetilde{\beta}_{0},\widetilde{\beta}_{2})
for the least squares estimator of
(\beta_{0},\beta_{2})
based on
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{v}_{2}=\frac{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(Y_{i}-\widetilde{\beta}_{0}-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}Z_{i})^{2}}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{2}}(Y_{i}-\overline{Y}_{2})^{2}}\sim\frac{1}{n-1}F_{1,n-1}.

Since
F_{2,n-1}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\chi_{2}^{2}/2
and
F_{1,n-1}\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\chi_{1}^{2}
where
\chi_{k}^{2}
denotes the chi-square distribution with
k
degrees of freedom, we observe that

\displaystyle n(\widehat{v}_{1}-\widehat{v}_{2})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}2U-2V,

where
U
and
V
are independent (due to the sample splitting) with
U\sim\chi_{2}^{2}
and
V\sim\chi_{1}^{2}
. It turns out that the denominator of
T_{\mathrm{W}}
can also affect the limiting behaviour of
T_{\mathrm{W}}
non-trivially in this degenerate situation, and the exact form of the limiting distribution relies on the choice of
\widehat{\eta}_{1}
and
\widehat{\eta}_{2}
. Nonetheless, we can still argue that the limiting distribution is not Gaussian. To see this, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{P}(T_{\mathrm{W}}<0)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{P}(U-V<0)=\mathbb{P}_{P}(F_{2,1}<1/2)\neq\Phi(0).

Therefore, when
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}=0
, the test based on
T_{\mathrm{W}}
can be either conservative or anti-conservative depending on the choice of
\alpha
.

We remark that our result in Section
S3.1

did not assume linear models, and it also applies to the practical approach suggested by
Williamson et al., 2023
using cross-fitting. Here, on the other hand, we consider a linear model because it allows us to show explicitly that the asymptotic distribution of
T_{W}
is non-Gaussian, so the test is not asymptotically valid.
As acknowledged in
Williamson et al., 2023
, we believe that the same validity issue arises when nonparametric procedures are considered, potentially using cross-fitting. However, the argument becomes unnecessarily complicated so we do not pursue this direction here.

S3.3
Linear model comparison

To provide an empirical comparison of the local power properties of the PCM with the approach considered in
Williamson et al., 2021
;
Williamson et al., 2023
and the more conventional
F
-test with robust standard error
(
White, 1980
)
(as implemented in the R package
lmtest

(
Zeileis and Hothorn, 2002
)
), we consider the following setup where
Z
and
\xi
are independent
N_{5}(0,\boldsymbol{I})
random vectors,
\varepsilon\sim N(0,1)
independently of
Z
and
\xi
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(1,2,3,4,5)/\sqrt{n}
and

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=Z+\xi,

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}X+2\Bigl(\bigl(1+e^{-3X_{1}}\bigr)^{-1}+\bigl(1+e^{-3Z_{1}}\bigr)^{-1}\Bigr)\varepsilon.

We simulate
n\in\{100,400,1600,6400\}
observations from the above model. All regressions are performed using OLS, except
\widehat{v}
which uses a random forest and we only apply Algorithm
1
for simplicity (rather than the derandomised version). We apply the
wgsc
in two different ways. The test labelled
wgsc
follows
Williamson et al., 2023
as in the rest of the paper. The test labelled
wgsc_no_x
is still employs sample-splitting to compute the test but no cross-fitting is done to compute the regression estimators. The resulting test remains valid as the Donsker conditions are satisfied.

The results can be seen in Figure
S1
.
They confirm our theoretical observations in Section
S3
on the power properties of the PCM and
wgsc
tests in this linear model setting.

Figure S1:
Power in the alternative settings considered in Section
S3.3
for nominal 5%-level tests.

Appendix S4
Splines

This section is a self-contained description of spline spaces and some of their properties relevant for the spline regressions in Section
5
. The definitions given here are not standard in the spline literature, in the sense that they are less general than the usual definitions, but they suffice for the purposes of regression with splines. One particular simplification that we will adhere to throughout is to restrict attention to splines with equi-spaced knots that are defined on the unit hypercube.

We start by considering function spaces of piecewise polynomials with adjustable degrees of smoothness and give a definition of uniform B-splines.

Definition S19
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and let
\Delta=(\Delta_{\ell})_{\ell=0}^{N+1}
be the knots of an equi-spaced partition of
[0,1]
, with
\Delta_{0}:=0
and
\Delta_{N+1}:=1
. For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, define the
spline space

\mathcal{S}_{r,N}
to be the set of functions
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, where the restriction of
f
to
[\Delta_{\ell-1},\Delta_{\ell}]
is a polynomial of degree at most
r-1
for
\ell\in[N+1]
and where
f
is
(r-2)
-times continuously differentiable when
r\geq 2
(we interpret this as meaning ‘continuous’ when
r=2
). We say that
r
is the
order
of
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}
. Define the vector
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{N+2r})\in[0,1]^{N+2r}
by

t:=(\underbrace{\Delta_{0},\dots,\Delta_{0}}_{r},\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{N}\underbrace{\Delta_{N+1},\dots,\Delta_{N+1}}_{r}).

For
s\in[r]
and
k\in[N+2r-s]
, define the functions
B_{k,s,N}
recursively for
x\in[0,1)
by

B_{k,1,N}(x):=\mathbbm{1}_{[t_{k},t_{k+1})}(x),

and, for
s\in\{2,\ldots,r\}
,

\displaystyle B_{k,s,N}(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{x-t_{k}}{t_{k+s-1}-t_{k}}B_{k,s-1,N}(x)+\frac{t_{k+s}-x}{t_{k+s}-t_{k+1}}B_{k+1,s-1,N}(x)

(with the convention that
0/0:=0
). We also define
B_{k,s,N}(1):=\lim_{x\nearrow 1}B_{k,s,N}(x)
. The
K:=N+r
functions
B_{1,r,N},\ldots,B_{K,r,N}
are called
B-splines
.

It is standard in the spline literature to parametrise spline spaces in terms of the order
r
of the polynomials rather than the degree (
r-1
). The Curry–Schoenberg theorem gives a relationship between the two definitions above.

Proposition S20

(Curry–Schoenberg)
.

The set of B-splines
\{B_{k,r,N}\}_{k=1}^{K}
is a basis for
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}
.

Proof.

See
Schumaker, 2007
.
∎

It is worth noting some properties of B-splines and the spline space
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}
.

Proposition S21
.

(a)

The B-splines
\{B_{k,r,N}\}_{k=1}^{K}
are non-negative and form a partition of unity; i.e.,

\sum_{k=1}^{K}B_{k,r,N}(x)=1.

for all
x\in[0,1]
.

(b)

For any
f\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}
of the form
f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}B_{k,r,N}(x)
with
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, there exists
c_{s}(r)>0
, depending only on
r
, such that

c_{s}(r)K^{-1/p}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{p}\leq\|f\|_{p}\leq 2^{1/p}K^{-1/p}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{p}.

(S39)

for all
p\in[1,\infty]
. In particular,

c_{s}(r)K^{-1/p}\leq\|B_{k,r,N}\|_{p}\leq 2^{1/p}K^{-1/p}

for all
k\in[K]
.

Proof.

(a)
This follows from Equations (4.5) and (4.10) of
de Boor, 1976
.

(b)
The conclusion of
de Boor, 1976
yields the existence of
c_{s}(r)>0
such that

c_{s}(r)(N+1)^{-1/p}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{p}\leq\|f\|_{p}\leq r^{-1/p}(N+1)^{-1/p}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{p}.

(S40)

But
(N+1)^{-1/p}\geq K^{-1/p}
since
K=N+r
, yielding the lower bound in (
S39
). For the upper bound in (
S39
), we note that

K\leq 2\max(N,r)\leq 2(N+1)r

(S41)

and rearranging yields the desired result.
∎

We will require splines on
[0,1]^{d}
instead of just
[0,1]
, and to that end we tensorise our earlier spline constructions.

Definition S22
.

Recall Definition
S19
. Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and define the
d
-tensor spline space

\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}:=\biggl\{f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R},f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=\prod_{j=1}^{d}f_{j}(x_{j}):f_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}\ \forall j\in[d]\biggr\}.

Let
\otimes
denote the vectorised outer product operator, so that
x\otimes y:=\mathrm{vec}(xy^{\top})
for Euclidean vectors
x
and
y
, where
\mathrm{vec}
denotes the vectorisation operator. Let
\boldsymbol{B}_{r,N}:[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N+r}
have
k
th component function
B_{k,r,N}
, so that
\boldsymbol{B}_{r,N}(x)=\bigl(B_{1,r,N}(x),\ldots,B_{N+r,r,N}(x)\bigr)^{\top}
. Now redefine
K:=(N+r)^{d}
; since
\otimes
is associative, we may define tensor-basis functions
\boldsymbol{\phi}\equiv\boldsymbol{\phi}_{r,N}:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}
by

\boldsymbol{\phi}(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\equiv\bigl(\phi_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}),\ldots,\phi_{K}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\bigr)^{\top}:=\boldsymbol{B}_{r,N}(x_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes\boldsymbol{B}_{r,N}(x_{d}).

By properties of the tensor product and the Curry–Schoenberg theorem, the collection
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
forms a basis for the
d
-tensor spline space
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
under the usual pointwise addition and scalar multiplication operations, and we refer to it as the
d
-tensor B-spline basis of
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
. In our subsequent asymptotic results, the first of which is Lemma
S28
, we will think of
d
and
r
as fixed, but allow
N
(and consequently
K
) to depend on
n
. Proposition
S23
below shows that the properties of univariate B-splines given in Proposition
S21
carry over to the
d
-tensor splines.

Proposition S23
.

(a)

The basis functions
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
are non-negative and form a partition of unity.

(b)

For any
f\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{r,N}
of the form
f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\phi_{k}(x)
where
\boldsymbol{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{K})\in\mathbb{R}^{K}
, and for any
p\in[1,\infty]
,

c_{s}(r)^{d}K^{-1/p}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{p}\leq\|f\|_{p}\leq 2^{d/p}K^{-1/p}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{p},

where
c_{s}(r)>0
is taken from Proposition
S21
(b).
In particular, for all
k\in[K]
,

c_{s}(r)^{d}K^{-1/p}\leq\|\phi_{k}\|_{p}\leq 2^{d/p}K^{-1/p}.

(c)

For any
Z
with distribution
P
on
[0,1]^{d}
, the matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z)\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z)^{\top}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
satisfies

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})\leq K^{-1}

and

\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})\geq K^{-2}.

(d)

Now suppose that
P
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]^{d}
with density
p
. If
C:=\sup_{z\in[0,1]^{d}}p(z)<\infty
, then

\lambda_{\max}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\bigr)\leq C2^{d}K^{-1}.

If instead
c:=\inf_{z\in[0,1]^{d}}p(z)>0
, then

\lambda_{\min}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\bigr)\geq cc_{s}(r)^{d}K^{-1},

where
c_{s}(r)>0
is taken from (b).

Proof.

(a)
This follows from Proposition
S21
(a)
and the definition of the
d
-tensor B-spline basis.

(b)

We will only prove the case
d=2
, since the full result will then follow by induction on
d
. For the lower bound, we can write

f(x_{1},x_{2})=\sum_{k=1}^{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\sqrt{K}}\beta_{k\ell}B_{k,r,N}(x_{1})B_{\ell,r,N}(x_{2})=:\sum_{k=1}^{\sqrt{K}}\gamma_{k}(x_{2})B_{k,r,N}(x_{1}).

For
p\in[1,\infty)
, we have by using (
S40
) twice that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{p}^{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\biggl|\sum_{k=1}^{\sqrt{K}}\gamma_{k}(x_{2})B_{k,r,N}(x_{1})\biggr|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}\geq\frac{c_{s}(r)^{p}}{N+1}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{k=1}^{\sqrt{K}}|\gamma_{k}(x_{2})|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c_{s}(r)^{2p}}{(N+1)^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\sqrt{K}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\sqrt{K}}|\beta_{k\ell}|^{p}=\frac{c_{s}(r)^{2p}}{(N+1)^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|^{p}_{p}\geq\frac{c_{s}(r)^{2p}}{K^{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|^{p}_{p},

as desired. For
p=\infty
, we have by a similar argument that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x_{1},x_{2}\in[0,1]}\biggl|\sum_{k=1}^{\sqrt{K}}\gamma_{k}(x_{2})B_{k,r,N}(x_{1})\biggr|\geq c_{s}(r)\sup_{x_{2}\in[0,1]}\max_{k\in[\sqrt{K}]}|\gamma_{k}(x_{2})|

\displaystyle\geq c_{s}(r)^{2}\max_{k,\ell\in[\sqrt{K}]}|\beta_{k\ell}|=c_{s}(r)^{2}\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{\infty},

again as desired.
For the upper bound, we argue very similarly, and use the fact that, with
K
redefined as
(N+r)^{d}
, we have
K\leq 2^{d}(N+1)^{d}r^{d}
by (
S41
).

(c)
Note that

K\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})\leq{\mathrm{tr}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})={\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi_{k}^{2}(Z)\biggr)\leq{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\biggl\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi_{k}(Z)\biggr\}^{2}\biggr)=1,

and by Cauchy–Schwarz,

K\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})\geq{\mathrm{tr}}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})={\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi_{k}^{2}(Z)\biggr)\geq\frac{1}{K}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\biggl\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\phi_{k}(Z)\biggr\}^{2}\biggr)=\frac{1}{K}.

(d)
We have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}=1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\boldsymbol{\beta}=\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}=1}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\biggl\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\phi_{k}(Z)\biggr\}^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}=1}\biggl\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\phi_{k}\biggr\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2^{d}C}{K},

where the final inequality uses
(b)
. By a similar argument,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})

\displaystyle=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}=1}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\boldsymbol{\beta}=\inf_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}=1}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl(\biggl\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\phi_{k}(Z)\biggr\}^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\geq c\inf_{\boldsymbol{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{K}:\|\boldsymbol{\beta}\|_{2}=1}\biggl\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{k}\phi_{k}\biggr\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{c}{c_{s}(r)^{d}K},

as required.
∎

We will now argue that splines are strong approximators over classes of sufficiently smooth functions, as defined by Hölder smoothness:

Definition S24
.

Given a multi-index
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{d})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|:=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\alpha_{j}
and an
|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|
-times differentiable function
f:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we define

D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f:=\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{1}}}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}}\dots\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{d}}}{\partial x_{d}^{\alpha_{d}}}f.

For
s>0
, write
s_{0}:=\left\lceil s\right\rceil-1
and define
\mathcal{H}_{s}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{s}^{d}
to be the set
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
that are
s_{0}
-times differentiable and that satisfy

\max_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=s_{0}}|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f(x)-D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f(\tilde{x})|\leq C\|x-\tilde{x}\|_{2}^{s-s_{0}}\quad\forall x,\tilde{x}\in[0,1]^{d}

(S42)

and

\max_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}:|\boldsymbol{\alpha}|=s_{0}}\|D^{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}f\|_{\infty}\leq C

(S43)

for some
C>0
. If
f\in\mathcal{H}_{s}
, then the infimum of the set of
C>0
for which both (
S42
) and (
S43
) hold is called the
s
-Hölder norm, and is denoted by
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}
.

The following basic result shows that given normed space of real-valued functions on
[0,1]^{d}
containing
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
, we can find a best approximant within
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
.

Lemma S25
.

Let
(\mathcal{V},\|\cdot\|)
denote a normed space of real-valued functions on
[0,1]^{d}
that contains
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
as a subspace. Then given any
f\in\mathcal{V}
, there exists
f^{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
such that
\|f-f^{*}\|=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}}\|f-g\|
. If
\|\cdot\|
is strictly convex, then this best approximant is unique.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
is a finite-dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{V}
,
Powell, 1981
guarantees the existence of the best approximant
f^{*}
. The uniqueness property follows from
Powell, 1981
since
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
is convex.
∎

The approximation properties of splines over Hölder smoothness classes are characterised below.

Proposition S26
.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{H}_{s}^{d}
. Then there exists
C(d,r)>0
and
f^{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
such that

\|f-f^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{C(d,r)}{(N+1)^{\min(s,r)}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}\leq\frac{C(d,r)}{(2rK)^{\min(s,r)/d}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}.

(S44)

Proof.

Given
g:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
j\in[d]
,
h>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
k
th forward difference of
g
in coordinate
j
with spacing
h
at
x
by

\Delta_{j,h}^{k}g(x):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}(-1)^{k-\ell}\binom{k}{\ell}g(x+\ell he_{j}),

where
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the
j
th standard basis vector. The
k
th modulus of smoothness of
g
in coordinate
j
of radius
t\in(0,1/k]
is then defined as

\omega_{j}^{k}(g;t):=\sup_{h\in[0,t]}\sup_{x\in[0,1-kh]}|\Delta_{j,h}^{k}g(x)|.

By Lemma
S25
and
Schumaker, 2007
, there exists
f^{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
such that

\|f-f^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq C^{\prime}(d,r)\sum_{j=1}^{d}\omega_{j}^{r}\bigl(f;1/(N+1)\bigr),

for some
C^{\prime}(d,r)>0
depending only on
d
and
r
.
First consider the case
r\geq s
, and recall the notation
s_{0}:=\lceil s\rceil-1
. By
Schumaker, 2007
,

\displaystyle\omega_{j}^{r}\bigl(f;1/(N+1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(N+1)^{s_{0}}}\omega_{j}^{r-s_{0}}\bigl(D^{s_{0}e_{j}}f;1/(N+1)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{r-s_{0}-1}}{(N+1)^{s_{0}}}\omega_{j}^{1}\bigl(D^{s_{0}e_{j}}f;1/(N+1)\bigr)\leq\frac{2^{r-1}}{(N+1)^{s}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}.

On the other hand, if
r<s
, then by
Schumaker, 2007
,

\omega_{j}^{r}\bigl(f;1/(N+1)\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{(N+1)^{r}}\|D^{re_{j}}f\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{(N+1)^{r}}\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}_{s}}.

Combining these bounds yields the first inequality in (
S44
) with
C(d,r):=2^{r-1}dC^{\prime}(d,r)
. The final bound again follows from the fact that
K\leq 2^{d}(N+1)^{d}r^{d}
.
∎

Our next result provides a way of translating properties between population least squares approximants and supremum norm approximants.

Proposition S27
.

Let
Z
be a random vector taking values in
[0,1]^{d}
, and let
\mathcal{F}
be a class of functions
f:[0,1]^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbb{E}\bigl(f(Z)^{2}\bigr)<\infty
for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
. Then for each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, there exists a unique
f^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
such that

{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{f(Z)-f^{\dagger}(Z)\}^{2}\bigr)=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{S}^{d}_{r,N}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(\{f(Z)-g(Z)\}^{2}\bigr).

Now fix
f\in\mathcal{F}
and
f^{*}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
. Further, suppose that
Z
has a density
p
with respect to Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]^{d}
satisfying
c:=\inf_{z\in[0,1]^{d}}p(z)>0
and
C:=\sup_{z\in[0,1]^{d}}p(z)<\infty
. Then there exists
M(c,C,d,r)>0
such that

\|f-f^{\dagger}\|_{\infty}\leq M(c,C,d,r)\|f-f^{*}\|_{\infty}.

Proof.

Let
P
denote the distribution of
Z
, and let
L_{2}(P)
denote the normed space of equivalence classes of measurable functions
g:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

\|g\|_{2,P}:=\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\bigl(g(Z)^{2}\bigr)\bigr\}^{1/2}<\infty

under the binary relation where
g\sim g^{\circ}
if
g(Z)=g^{\circ}(Z)
almost surely
1
1

1

We do not distinguish between a function with finite
\|\cdot\|_{2,P}
norm and its equivalence class in what follows.
. The existence of the unique
f^{\dagger}\in\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
follows from Lemma
S25
since the
L_{2}(P)
norm is strictly convex.

Now define
\tilde{g}:=f-f^{*}
, so the unique
L_{2}(P)
-best approximant
\tilde{g}^{\dagger}
to
\tilde{g}
in
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
is given by
\tilde{g}^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}-f^{*}
.
We now verify that Conditions A.1, A.2 and A.3 of
Huang, 2003
hold. Condition A.1 is satisfied by our hypotheses on
c,C
; Condition A.2 holds since the knots of the splines in
\mathcal{S}^{d}_{r,N}
are equi-spaced; and Condition A.3 is satisfied by Proposition
S23
(b), where we again use the bounds
(N+1)^{d}\leq K\leq 2^{d}(N+1)^{d}r^{d}
. Thus,
Huang, 2003
yields the existence of
M^{\prime}(c,C,d,r)>0
such that

\|\tilde{g}^{\dagger}\|_{\infty}\leq M^{\prime}(c,C,d,r)\|\tilde{g}\|_{\infty}.

We conclude that

\|f-f^{\dagger}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\tilde{g}\|_{\infty}+\|\tilde{g}^{\dagger}\|_{\infty}\leq\bigl(1+M^{\prime}(c,C,d,r)\bigr)\|\tilde{g}\|_{\infty},

so the desired result holds with
M(c,C,d,r):=1+M^{\prime}(c,C,d,r)
.
∎

Lemma
S28
below will ensure that, provided
K
increases slightly slower than
n
(so that
K\log(K)/n\rightarrow 0
), performing ordinary least squares with the
d
-tensor B-spline basis will yield consistent estimators.

Lemma S28
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
denote a family of distributions for a random vector
Z
taking values in
[0,1]^{d}
, and let
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of independent and identically distributed copies of
X
. Recall the notation
\boldsymbol{\phi}=(\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{K})^{\top}
, where
\{\phi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
denotes the
d
-tensor B-spline basis of
\mathcal{S}_{r,N}^{d}
. For
P\in\mathcal{P}
, define
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}:={\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z)\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z)^{\top}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times K}
and
\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}:=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z_{i})\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z_{i})^{\top}
. Suppose that each
P\in\mathcal{P}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]^{d}
, with corresponding density
p_{P}
satisfying
C:=\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sup_{z\in[0,1]^{d}}p_{P}(z)<\infty
. Then

K\bigl\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\biggl(\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}}\biggr).

If, in addition,
c:=\inf_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\inf_{z\in[0,1]^{d}}p_{P}(z)>0
and
K\log(K)/n\to 0
, then

K^{-1}\bigl\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}^{-1}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathcal{P}}\biggl(\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}}\biggr),

and

\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(K^{-1}),\quad\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathcal{P}}(K).

(S45)

Proof.

For the first claim, by Markov’s inequality, it suffices to show that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}K{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\bigl\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)=O\biggl(\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}}\biggr)

as
n\to\infty
. By the Rudelson law of large numbers for matrices
(
Belloni et al., 2015
, Lemma 6.2)
(and Chebyshev’s inequality when
K=1
), there exists a universal constant
C_{*}>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}K{\mathbb{E}}_{P}\bigl(\bigl\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{*}K\log(eK)}{n}+C_{*}\sqrt{\frac{K^{2}\log(eK)}{n}}\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}\sqrt{\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{*}K\log(eK)}{n}+C_{*}\sqrt{C2^{d}}\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}},

since
\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z)\|_{2}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\phi}(Z)\|_{1}=1
and
\|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C2^{d}K^{-1}
, by Proposition
S23
(a) and (d) respectively.

For the second claim, note first that
K\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P})\geq cc_{s}(r)=:b>0
by Proposition
S23
(d).
From the first claim of the lemma and the hypothesis that
K\log(K)/n\rightarrow 0
, given
\epsilon>0
, we can choose
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
large enough that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(K\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\frac{b}{2}\biggr)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

for
n\geq n_{0}
. Then, by another application of the first claim of the lemma, by increasing
n_{0}
if necessary, we can find
M_{0}>0
such that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(K\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\frac{b^{2}M}{2}\biggl\{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}}\biggr\}\biggr)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}

for all
n\geq n_{0}
and
M\geq M_{0}
. It follows by Lemma
S16
that for
n\geq n_{0}
and
M\geq M_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}^{-1}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq M\biggl\{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}}\biggr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(K\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq\frac{b^{2}M}{2}\biggl\{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{K\log(eK)}{n}}\biggr\}\biggr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}}{\mathbb{P}}_{P}\biggl(K\|\boldsymbol{\widehat{\Sigma}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{P}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>\frac{b}{2}\biggr)\leq\epsilon,

which establishes the second claim.

Finally, by the first part of Proposition
S23
(d),
